Figure 0.B.1
:
Description of the history
h_{\varphi}
from Theorem
4.2
. Dashed edges only belong to a possible consistent witness of
h_{\varphi}
, where we assume
v_{1}^{0}=x_{k}
. Transaction
t_{1}^{0}
reads
v_{1}^{0},v_{1}^{1}
and
v_{1}^{2}
from round
0
; imposing some constraints on the transactions that write them. Due to axiom
RC
’s definition, transaction
t_{1}^{1}
must read
v_{1}^{1}
from
t_{1}^{0}
while transaction
t_{1}^{2}
must read
v_{1}^{1}
from
t_{1}^{1}
.

The proof is divided in four steps: Lemma
11
proves that the
h_{\varphi}
is a polynomial-size transformation of
\varphi
, Lemma
12
proves that the
h_{\varphi}
is indeed a history and Lemmas
13
and
17
prove that
h_{\varphi}
is consistent if and only if
\varphi
is
1
-in-
3
satisfiable.

Lemma 11

h_{\varphi}
is a polynomial size transformation on the length of
\varphi
.

Proof

If
\varphi
has
n
clauses and
m
variables,
h_{\varphi}
employs
6n+2m+1
transactions. As
m\leq 3n
,
|T|\in\mathcal{O}(n)
. The number of variables,
|\mathsf{Keys}|=m+|T|
, so
|\mathsf{Keys}|\in\mathcal{O}(n)
. As every transaction has at most two events,
|\mathsf{events}({h_{\varphi}})|\in\mathcal{O}(n)
. Moreover,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}\subseteq\mathsf{Keys}\times T\times T
and
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\subseteq T\times T
, so
|{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}|\in\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
and
|{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}|\in\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
. Thus,
h_{\varphi}
is a polynomial transformation of
\varphi
.

∎

For proving that
h_{\varphi}
is a history, by Definition
1
it suffices to prove that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
is an acyclic relation. Indeed, by our choice of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
, for every key
x
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}^{-1}
is a partial function that, whenever it is defined, associates reads to writes on
x
. Hence, from Lemma
12
we conclude that
h_{\varphi}
is a history.

Lemma 12

The relation
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
is acyclic.

Proof

For proving that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
is acyclic, we reason by induction on the number of clauses. In particular, we show that for every pair of transactions
t,t^{\prime}
if
\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})\leq i
and
(t,t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
, then
\mathtt{round}(t)\leq i
and
(t^{\prime},t)\not\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
.

•

Base case:
The base case refers to round
0
; which contains
init
and transactions
0_{j},1_{j},1\leq j\leq m
. We observe that transactions in round
0
do not contain any read event. Hence,
(t,t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
. In such case, the result immediately holds by construction of
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
.

•

Inductive case:
Let us suppose that the induction hypothesis holds for every
1\leq i\leq k\leq n
and let us prove it also for
k+1\leq n
. If
\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})<k+1
,
\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})\leq k
and the result holds by induction hypothesis; so we can assume without loss of generality that
\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})=k+1
. By construction of both
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
, if
(t,t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
,
\mathtt{round}(t)<\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})
. Hence,
\mathtt{round}(t)\leq k
. By induction hypothesis on
t
, if
(t^{\prime},t)\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
,
\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})\leq k<k+1=\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})
; which is impossible. Thus, we conclude that
(t^{\prime},t)\not\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
.

∎

Lemma 13

If
\varphi
is
1
-in-
3
satisfiable then
h_{\varphi}
is consistent.

Proof

Let
\alpha:\mathsf{Keys}\to\{0,1\}
an assignment that makes
1
-in-
3
satisfiable. To construct a witness of
h_{\varphi}
we define a write-read relation
\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}
that extends
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
and a total order on its transactions. For that, we first define a total order
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
between the transactions in
T
. In Equation
6
we define two auxiliary relations
\hat{\mathtt{r}}
and
\hat{\mathtt{b}}
based on
\alpha
that totally orders the transactions that belongs to the same round.

For every clause
C_{i},1\leq i\leq n
let
j_{i}
be the unique index s.t.
\alpha(v_{i}^{j_{i}})=1
. Such index allow us to order the transactions in the round
i
:
t_{i}^{j_{i}}
preceding
t_{i}^{j_{i}+1\bmod 3}
while
t_{i}^{j_{i}+1\bmod 3}
preceding
t_{i}^{j_{i}+2\bmod 3}
. Intuitively,
t_{i}^{j_{i}}
must precede the other two transactions in the total order as
v_{i}^{j}
is the variable that is satisfied. Then, we connect every pair of consecutive rounds thanks to relation
\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{1}
.

For transactions in round
0
, we enforce that transactions associated to the same variable are totally ordered using
\alpha
. In particular, for every
i,1\leq i\leq m
,
0_{i}
precedes
1_{i}
in
\hat{\mathtt{b}}
if and only if
\alpha(v_{i})=1
. Then, we connect every pair tuple in
\hat{\mathtt{b}}
with relation
\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{2}
. Finally, we connect
init
with transactions in round
0
as well as round
0
with round
1
thanks to relation
\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{3}
.

\displaystyle\hat{\mathtt{r}}=\left\{\left.\begin{array}[]{l}(t_{i}^{j_{i}},t_{i}^{j_{i}+1\bmod 3})\\
(t_{i}^{j_{i}+1\bmod 3},t_{i}^{j_{i}+2\bmod 3})\end{array}\right|\begin{array}[]{l}1\leq i\leq n,0\leq j_{i}\leq 2\\
\alpha(v_{i}^{j_{i}})=1\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\hat{\mathtt{b}}=\{(0_{i},1_{i})\ |\ x_{i}\in\mathsf{Keys}\land\alpha(x_{i})=1\}\cup\{(1_{i},0_{i})\ |\ x_{i}\in\mathsf{Keys}\land\alpha(x_{i})=0\}

\displaystyle\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{1}=\{(t_{i}^{j_{i}+2\bmod 3},t_{i+1}^{j_{i+1}})\ |\ 1\leq i<n,0\leq j_{i},j_{i+1}\leq 2,\alpha(v_{i}^{j_{i}})=1=\alpha(v_{i}^{j_{i+1}})\}

\displaystyle\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{2}=\{(1_{i},0_{j}),(1_{i},1_{j}),(0_{i},0_{j}),(0_{i},1_{j})\ |\ 1\leq i<j\leq m\}

\displaystyle\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{3}=\{(\textup{{{init}}},0_{1}),(\textup{{{init}}},1_{1})\}\cup\{(1_{m},t_{1}^{j_{1}}),(0_{m},t_{1}^{j_{1}})\ |\ 0\leq j_{1}\leq 2,\alpha(v_{i}^{j_{1}})=1\}

(11)

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}=(\hat{\mathtt{r}}\cup\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{1}\cup\hat{\mathtt{b}}\cup\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{2}\cup\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{3})^{+}
. The proof of
Lemma

13
concludes thanks to
Lemmas

14
and
15
,
Proposition

1
and
Corollary

1
. First,
Lemma

14
proves that the relation
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
is a total order between transactions. Then,
Lemma

15
shows that
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
allow us define
\overline{h}
, a witness of
h_{\varphi}
. And finally, with the aid of
Proposition

1
and
Corollary

1
we conclude that
\overline{h}
is consistent; so it is a consistent witness of
h_{\varphi}
.

∎

Lemma 14

The relation
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
is a total order.

Proof

For proving that
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
is a total order, we show by induction that if
(t,t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
and
\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})\leq i
, then
\mathtt{round}(t)\leq i
and
(t^{\prime},t)\not\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
.

•

Base case:
We observe that by construction of
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
,
t^{\prime}\neq\textup{{{init}}}
. We prove the base case by a second induction that if there exists
i^{\prime},1\leq i^{\prime}\leq m
s.t.
t^{\prime}\in\{0_{i^{\prime}},1_{i^{\prime}}\}
and
(t,t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
then either
t=\textup{{{init}}}
or there exists
i\leq i^{\prime}
s.t.
t\in\{0_{i},1_{i}\}
and
(t^{\prime},t)\not\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
.

–

Base case:
Let us suppose that
\alpha(x_{0})=1
as the other case is symmetric. If
t=\textup{{{init}}}
,
(t^{\prime},t)\not\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
as
init
is minimal in
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
. If not, then
t^{\prime}=1_{1}
and
t=0_{1}
. We conclude once more that
(t,t^{\prime})\not\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
as
0_{1}
only have
init
as a
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
-predecessor; which is
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
-minimal.

–

Induction hypothesis:
Let us suppose that the induction hypothesis holds for every
1\leq i\leq k\leq m
and let us prove it also for
k+1\leq m
. If
i^{\prime}<k
, we conclude the result by induction hypothesis; so we can assume that
i^{\prime}=k
. Moreover, as
init
is
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
-minimal, we can assume without loss of generality that
t\neq\textup{{{init}}}
. Thus, by construction of
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
, there must exists
i,1\leq i\leq m
s.t.
t\in\{0_{i},1_{i}\}
. In particular,
i\leq i^{\prime}
. Thus, if
i<i^{\prime}
and
(t^{\prime},t)
would be in
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
, by induction hypothesis on
t
we would deduce that
i^{\prime}\leq i<i^{\prime}
; which is impossible. Hence, we can assume that
i^{\prime}=i
. Let us assume that
\alpha(x_{i})=1
as the other case is symmetric. Thus, we deduce that
t=0_{i}
and
t^{\prime}=1_{i}
. We observe that
(t^{\prime},t)\not\in\hat{\mathtt{r}}\cup\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{1}\cup\hat{\mathtt{b}}\cup\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{2}\cup\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{3}
. As
T
is finite, if
(t^{\prime},t)\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
, there would exist a transaction
t^{\prime\prime}\neq t^{\prime}
s.t
(t^{\prime\prime},t)\in\hat{\mathtt{r}}\cup\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{1}\cup\hat{\mathtt{b}}\cup\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{2}\cup\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{3}
and
(t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
. But in such case, either
t^{\prime\prime}=\textup{{{init}}}
or there would exists an integer
i^{\prime\prime},1\leq i^{\prime\prime}<i\leq m
s.t.
t^{\prime\prime}\in\{0_{i^{\prime\prime}},1_{i^{\prime\prime}}\}
; which is impossible by induction hypothesis. In conclusion,
(t^{\prime},t)\not\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
.

•

Inductive case:
Let us suppose that the induction hypothesis holds for every
1\leq i\leq k\leq n
and let us prove it also for
k+1\leq n
. Let thus
t,t^{\prime}
transactions s.t.
\mathtt{round}(t)^{\prime}\leq k+1
and
(t,t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
. If
\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})<k+1
,
\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})\leq k
and the result holds by induction hypothesis; so we can assume without loss of generality that
\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})=k+1
. By construction of
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
,
\mathtt{round}(t)\leq k+1
. If
\mathtt{round}(t)\leq k
and
(t^{\prime},t)\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
, by induction hypothesis on
t
we obtain that
\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})\leq k<k+1=\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})
; which is impossible. Thus, we can also assume without loss of generality that
\mathtt{round}(t)=k+1
. In such case, we observe that
\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{1}
,
\hat{\mathtt{b}}
and
\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{1}
do not order transactions belonging to the same round. Hence if
(t,t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
and
(t^{\prime},t)\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
, we deduce that
(t,t^{\prime})\in\hat{\mathtt{r}}
and
(t^{\prime},t)\in\hat{\mathtt{r}}
. However, by construction of
\hat{\mathtt{r}}
, this is impossible, so we conclude once more that
(t^{\prime},t)\not\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
.

∎

Next, we construct a full history
\overline{h}
using
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
that extends
h_{\varphi}
. For every key
x
and
\mathtt{read}
event
r
, we define
w_{x}^{r}
as follows:

w_{x}^{r}=\max_{{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}}\{t\in T\ |\ {t}\ \mathsf{writes}\ {x}\land(t,r)\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}\}

(12)

Observe that
w_{x}^{r}
is well-defined as
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
is a total order and
init
write every key. For each key
x\in\mathtt{var}(\varphi)
, we define the relation
\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}=\{(w_{x}^{r},r)\ |\ r\in\mathsf{reads}({h})\}
. Then, we define the relation
\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}=\bigcup_{x\in\mathtt{var}(\varphi)}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
as well as the history
\overline{h}={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}})}
. Lemma
15
proves that
\overline{h}
is indeed a full history while Lemma
16
shows that
\overline{h}
is a witness of
h_{\varphi}
.

Lemma 15

\overline{h}
is a full history.

Proof

For showing that
\overline{h}
is a full history it suffices to show that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}
is acyclic. As
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
is a total order and
\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
, proving that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
concludes the result.
First, we prove that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
. Let
t,t^{\prime}
be transactions s.t.
(t,t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
. In such case,
\mathtt{round}(t)\leq\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})
; and they only coincide if
\mathtt{round}(t)=\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})=0
. Three cases arise:

•

\mathtt{round}(t)=\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})=0
:
As
(t,t^{\prime})\in\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{2}
, we conclude that
(t,t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
.

•

\mathtt{round}(t),\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})>0
:
As
\mathtt{round}(t),\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})>0
and
\mathtt{round}(t)\leq\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})
, by construction of
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
we deduce that
\mathtt{round}(t)<\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})
. As
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
is transitive, we can assume without loss of generality that
\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})=\mathtt{round}(t)+1
. Therefore, there exists
i,j,1\leq i<n,0\leq j\leq 2
s.t.
t=t_{i}^{j}
and
t^{\prime}=t_{i+1}^{j}
. Let
j_{i},j_{i+1},0\leq j_{i},j_{i+1}\leq 2
be the integers s.t.
\alpha(v_{i}^{j_{i}})=1=\alpha(v_{i+1}^{j_{i+1}})
. In such case, we know that
(t_{i}^{j},t_{i}^{j_{i}+2\bmod 3})\in\hat{\mathtt{r}}^{*}
,
(t_{i}^{j_{i}+2\bmod 3},t_{i+1}^{j_{i}})\in\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{1}
and
(t_{i+1}^{j_{i+1}},t_{i+1}^{j+1})\in\hat{\mathtt{r}}^{*}
. Hence, as
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
is transitive,
(t,t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
.

•

\mathtt{round}(t)=0
,
\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})>0
:
In this case, as
\mathtt{round}(t)=0
, there exists
i,1\leq i\leq m
s.t.
x_{i}\in\mathtt{var}(\varphi),t\in\{0_{i},1_{i}\}
. We assume without loss of generality that
t=1_{i}
as the other case is symmetric. In addition, as
\mathtt{round}(t^{\prime})>0
and
(t,t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
, there exists
i,1\leq i\leq n
s.t.
t^{\prime}=t_{i}^{0}
. We rely on the two previous proven cases to deduce the result: as
(0_{i},0_{m})\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
,
(0_{m},t_{0}^{j_{0}})\in\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{3}
,
(t_{0}^{j_{0}},t_{0}^{0})\in\hat{\mathtt{r}}^{*}
and
(t_{0}^{0},t_{i}^{0})\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
, we conclude that
(t,t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
.

Next, we prove that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
. Let
r
be a read event and
w
be a write event s.t.
(w,r)\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
. Then, there exists
i,i^{\prime},1\leq i<i^{\prime}\leq n
and
j,j^{\prime},0\leq j,j^{\prime}\leq 2
s.t.
w=t_{i}^{j}
and
\mathsf{tr}(r)=t_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
. Let
j_{i^{\prime}-1},j_{i^{\prime}},0\leq j_{i^{\prime}-1},j_{i^{\prime}}\leq 2
be the integers s.t.
\alpha(v_{i^{\prime}-1}^{j_{i^{\prime}-1}})=1=\alpha(v_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}})
. In such case, we know that
(t_{i}^{j},t_{i^{\prime}-1}^{j})\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}^{*}
,
(t_{i^{\prime}-1}^{j},t_{i^{\prime}-1}^{j_{i^{\prime}}+2\bmod 3})\in\hat{\mathtt{r}}^{*}
,
(t_{i^{\prime}-1}^{j_{i^{\prime}}+2\bmod 3},t_{i^{\prime}}^{j_{i}})\in\hat{\mathtt{c}}_{1}
and
(t_{i^{\prime}}^{j_{i^{\prime}}},t_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}})\in\hat{\mathtt{r}}^{*}
. As
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
is transitive, we conclude that
(w,r)\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
.

∎

We show that
\overline{h}
is indeed a full history, that is a witness of
h_{\varphi}
and that also witness
h_{\varphi}
’s consistency.

Lemma 16

The history
\overline{h}
is a witness of
h_{\varphi}
.

Proof

By Lemma
15

\overline{h}
is a full history. Hence, for proving that
\overline{h}
is a witness of
h_{\varphi}
, we need to show that for every key
x\in\mathsf{Keys}
and every read
r
, if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}^{-1}(r)\uparrow
,
\mathtt{WHERE}(r)(\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(w_{x}^{r},x))=0
. Note that by construction of
h_{\varphi}
, such cases coincide with
x\in\mathtt{var}(\varphi)
. In addition, we observe that if
r
is a read event, there exists indices
1\leq i\leq n,0\leq j\leq 2
s.t.
r\in t_{i}^{j}
. Thus, by Equation
6
, we only need to prove that
\mathtt{WHERE}(r)(\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(w_{x}^{r},x))=0
whenever
x
is
v_{i}^{0},v_{i}^{1}
or
v_{i}^{2}
.

We prove as an intermediate step that in each round, every key has the same value in
\overline{h}
. For every round
i
and key
x\in\mathtt{var}(\varphi)
, we consider the transaction
t_{x}^{i}=\max_{{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}}\{t\ |\ {t}\ \mathsf{writes}\ {x}\land\mathtt{round}(t)\leq i\}
. We prove by induction on the number of the round that
\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{x}^{i},x)=\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{x}^{0},x)=(x,\alpha(x))
.

•

Base case
: The base case,
i=0
, is immediately trivial. Note that in this case
\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{x}^{0},x)=\alpha(x)
.

•

Inductive case
: Let us assume that
\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{x}^{i-1},x)=\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{x}^{0},x)
and let us prove that
\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{x}^{i},x)=(x,\alpha(x))
. Note that in round
i
only keys
v_{i}^{0},v_{i}^{1}
and
v_{i}^{2}
are written; so for every other key
x
,
t_{x}^{i}=t_{x}^{i-1}
and by induction hypothesis,
\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{x}^{i},x)=(x,\alpha(x))
. Let thus
j,0\leq j\leq 2
s.t.
\alpha(v_{i}^{j})=1
. In this case,
t_{v_{i}^{j}}^{i}=t_{v_{i}^{j+2\bmod 3}}^{i}=t_{i}^{j+2\bmod 3}
and
t_{v_{i}^{j+1\bmod 3}}^{i}=t_{i}^{j+1\bmod 3}
. Hence, we can conclude the inductive step as:

\displaystyle\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{i}^{j+2\bmod 3},v_{i}^{j})=(v_{i}^{j},1)=(v_{i}^{j},\alpha(v_{i}^{j}))

\displaystyle\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{i}^{j+1\bmod 3},v_{i}^{j+1\bmod 3})=(v_{i}^{j+1\bmod 3},0)=(v_{i}^{j+1\bmod 3},\alpha(v_{i}^{j+1\bmod 3}))

\displaystyle\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{i}^{j+2\bmod 3},v_{i}^{j+2\bmod 3})=(v_{i}^{j+2\bmod 3},0)=(v_{i}^{j+2\bmod 3},\alpha(v_{i}^{j+2\bmod 3}))

We can thus conclude that
\overline{h}
is a witness of
h_{\varphi}
. Let
i,j,1\leq i\leq n,0\leq j\leq 2
be indices s.t.
\alpha(v_{i}^{j})=1
. For simplifying notation, we denote by
t_{0},t_{1},t_{2}
to the transactions
t_{i}^{j},t_{i}^{j+1\bmod 3}
and
t_{i}^{j+2\bmod 3}
respectively. We also denote by
r_{0},r_{1},r_{2}
to the read events that belong to
t_{0},t_{1}
and
t_{2}
respectively as well by
v_{0},v_{1},v_{2}
to the keys associated to
t_{0},t_{1}
and
t_{2}
respectively. For every key
x\neq v_{0},v_{1},v_{2}
and for every transaction
t
that writes
x
,
\mathtt{WHERE}(r_{j})(\mathtt{value}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}(t,x))=0,0\leq j\leq 2
; so we can focus only on keys
v_{0},v_{1}
and
v_{2}
. Three cases arise:

•

Transaction
t_{0}
:
Let thus
x
be a key in
\{v_{0},v_{1},v_{2}\}
. By construction of
h_{\varphi}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
,
t_{0}
reads
x
from
t_{x}^{i-1}
. As proved before,
\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{x}^{i-1},x)=(x,\alpha(x))
and
\alpha(x)=(x,1)
if and only if
x=v_{0}
. Hence, as
\mathtt{WHERE}(r_{0})(\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{x}^{i-1},x))=0
we conclude that
\mathtt{WHERE}(r_{0})(\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(w_{x}^{r_{0}},x))=0
.

•

Transaction
t_{1}
:
In this case,
t_{1}
reads
v_{2}
from
t_{v_{2}}^{i-1}
and it reads
v_{0}
and
v_{1}
from
t_{0}
. On one hand,
\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{v_{2}}^{i-1},v_{2})=(v_{2},\alpha(v_{2}))=(v_{2},0)
. Thus, as
\mathtt{WHERE}(r_{1})(t_{v_{2}}^{i-1})=0
, we conclude that
\mathtt{WHERE}(r_{1})(\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(w_{v_{2}}^{r_{1}},v_{2}))=0
. On the other hand, by construction of
h_{\varphi}
,
\mathtt{WHERE}(r_{1})(\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{0},v_{0}))=\mathtt{WHERE}(r_{1})(\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{0},v_{1}))=0
. Thus, the result hold.

•

Transaction
t_{2}
:
In this case,
t_{2}
read
v_{0}
from
t_{0}
and
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
from
t_{1}
. By construction of
h_{\varphi}
both
\mathtt{WHERE}(r_{2})(\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{0},v_{0}))
,
\mathtt{WHERE}(r_{2})(\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{1},v_{1}))
and
\mathtt{WHERE}(r_{2})(\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{1},v_{2}))
are equal to
0
; so we conclude the result.

∎

We conclude the proof showing that the execution
\xi={({\overline{h},{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}})}
is a consistent execution of
h_{\varphi}
. We observe that by construction of
\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
,
\overline{h}
satisfies
SER
using
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
.
Corollary

1
proves that
\mathsf{iso}(h_{\varphi})
is weaker than
SER
; which allow us to conclude that so
\overline{h}
satisfies
\mathsf{iso}(h_{\varphi})
. In other words, that
\overline{h}
is consistent.

Proposition 1

Let
h={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
be a full history,
\xi={({h,{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}})}
be an execution of
h
,
r
be a
\mathtt{read}
event,
t_{2}
be a transaction distinct from
\mathsf{tr}(r)
,
x
be a key and
\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{vis}(\mathsf{iso}(h)(\mathsf{tr}(e)))
. If
\mathsf{v}(t_{2},r,x)
holds in
\xi
, then
(t_{2},\mathsf{tr}(r))\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
.

Proof

The proposition is result of an immediate induction on the definition of
\mathsf{v}
. The base case is
{\color[rgb]{0.7695,0.1172,0.2266}\mathsf{po}},{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
, which holds by definition. The inductive case follows from the operators employed in Equation
3
: union, composition and transitive closure of relations; which are monotonic.

∎

As a consequence of
Proposition

1
and
Serializability
axiom definition, we obtain the following result.

Corollary 1

Any isolation level is weaker than
SER
.

Lemma 17

If
h_{\varphi}
is consistent then
\varphi
is
1
-in-
3
satisfiable.

Proof

If
h_{\varphi}
is consistent, there exists a consistent witness of
h_{\varphi}

\overline{h}={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}})}
. As
\overline{h}
is consistent and
\mathsf{iso}(h)
is stronger than
RC
, there exists a consistent execution of
\overline{h}
,
\xi={({\overline{h},{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}})}
. Let
\alpha_{h}:\mathtt{var}(\varphi)\to\{0,1\}
s.t. for every variable
v_{j},1\leq j\leq m
,
\alpha_{h}(v_{j})=1
if and only if
(0_{j},1_{j})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
. We show that
\varphi
is
1
-in-
3
satisfiable using
\alpha
.

As an intermediate step, we prove that
\alpha_{h}
describes the value read by any transaction in
\overline{h}
. For every
i,0\leq i\leq n
and key
x\in\mathtt{var}(\varphi)
, let
t_{x}^{i}=\max_{{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}}\{t\ |\ {t}\ \mathsf{writes}\ {x}\land\mathtt{round}(t)\leq i\}
. We prove by induction that for every
i,0\leq i\leq n
(1)
\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{x}^{i},x)=(x,\alpha(x))
, (2) for any
\mathtt{read}
event
r
from a transaction
t
s.t.
\mathtt{round}(t)\leq i
, if
(w,r)\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}
, then
w
coincides with
\max_{{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}}\{t\in T\ |\ {t}\ \mathsf{writes}\ {x}\land(t,\mathsf{tr}(r))\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}\}
and (3) if
i>0
,
\alpha(v_{i}^{j})=1
if and only if
(t_{i}^{j},t_{i}^{j+1\bmod 3})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
and
(t_{i}^{j+1\bmod 3},t_{i}^{j+2\bmod 3})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
.

•

Base case
: Let
j,1\leq j\leq m
be the integer s.t.
x=v_{j}
. In such case, (1) holds as
t_{x}^{0}=1_{j}
if and only if
\alpha(v_{j})=1
; and in such case,
\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{x}^{0},v_{j})=(v_{j},\alpha(v_{j}))
. Also (2) trivially holds as there is no
\mathtt{read}
event in a transaction belonging to round
0
. Finally, (3) also trivially holds as
i=0
.

•

Inductive case
: We assume that (1), (2) and (3) hold for round
i-1
and let us prove it for round
i
. Let
j
the index of the
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
-minimal transaction among
t_{i}^{1},t_{i}^{2},t_{i}^{3}
. We denote by
t_{0},t_{1},t_{2}
to
t_{i}^{j},t_{i}^{j+1\bmod 3}
and
t_{i}^{j+2\bmod 3}
respectively, by
r_{0},r_{1},r_{2}
to the unique
\mathtt{read}
event in
t_{0},t_{1}
and
t_{2}
respectively and by
v_{0},v_{1}
and
v_{2}
to the keys associated to
t_{0},t_{1}
and
t_{2}
respectively.

Let thus
x\in\mathtt{var}(\varphi)
be a key,
t
be a transaction among
t_{0},t_{1},t_{2}
and let
w_{x}^{t}
be a transaction s.t.
(w_{x}^{t},t)\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}
. As
\mathtt{round}(t_{x}^{i-1})<\mathtt{round}(t)
,
(t_{x}^{i-1},t)\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{t_{x}^{i-1}}
. Hence, as
\overline{h}
satisfies
RC
, either
w_{x}^{t}=t_{x}^{i-1}
or
\mathtt{round}(w_{x}^{t})=i
.

First we prove (3) analyzing
t_{0}
. As
(t_{0},t_{1})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
and
(t_{0},t_{2})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
and
\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
we deduce that
w_{x}^{t}=t_{x}^{i-1}
. In such case, as (1) holds by induction hypothesis and
\mathtt{WHERE}(r_{0})(\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{x}^{i-1},x))=0
, we conclude that
\alpha(x)=1
if
x=v_{0}
and
\alpha(x)=0
if
x=v_{1},v_{2}
.

For proving (2) we analyze three cases depending on
t
:

–

t=t_{0}
:
As proved before, if
t=t_{0}
,
w_{x}^{t}=t_{x}^{i-1}
. By definition of
t_{x}^{i-1}
, (2) holds.

–

t=t_{1}
:
As
t_{0}
only writes
v_{0}
and
v_{1}
and
(t_{1},t_{2})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
we deduce that for every key
x\neq v_{0},v_{1}
,
w_{x}^{t_{1}}=t_{x}^{i-1}
; which immediately implies (2). As (3) holds for round
i
, we know that
\alpha(v_{0})=1
and
\alpha(v_{1})=0
. Thus, if
x=v_{0},v_{1}
,
\mathtt{WHERE}(r_{2})(\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{x}^{i-1},x))=1
; so
(t_{x}^{i-1},t_{1})\not\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}
. In conclusion,
w_{x}^{t_{1}}=t_{0}
; which implies (2) by definition of
t_{0}
.

–

t=t_{2}
:
As
t_{0},t_{1}
only write
v_{0},v_{1}
and
v_{2}
we deduce that for every other key,
w_{x}^{t_{2}}=t_{x}^{i-1}
; which implies (2). Otherwise, we analyze the three sub-cases arising:

*

x=v_{2}
:
In this case,
t_{0}
does not write
v_{2}
; so there is only two options left,
t_{x}^{i-1}
and
t_{1}
. As (3) holds for round
i
,
\alpha(v_{2})=0
. Thus, as by induction hypothesis (2) holds for round
i-1
,
\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{v_{2}}^{i-1},v_{2})=(v_{2},0)
and hence,
\mathtt{WHERE}(r_{2})(\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{v_{2}}^{i-1},v_{2}))=1
. Therefore,
w_{v_{2}}^{t_{2}}
must be
t_{1}
; which implies (2).

*

x=v_{0}
:
Once again, there is only two possible options as
t_{1}
does not write
v_{0}
. As (3) holds for round
i
,
\alpha(v_{0})=1
. Thus, as by induction hypothesis (2) holds for round
i-1
,
\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{v_{0}}^{i-1},v_{0})=(v_{0},1)
and hence,
\mathtt{WHERE}(r_{2})(\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{v_{0}}^{i-1},v_{0}))=1
. Therefore,
w_{v_{0}}^{t_{2}}
must be
t_{0}
; which implies (2).

*

x=v_{1}
:
We observe in this case that
\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{0},v_{1})=(x,1)
; so
\mathtt{WHERE}(r_{2})(\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{0},v_{1}))=1
. Therefore, there is only two possible options,
t_{1}
and
t_{x}^{i-1}
. As
\overline{h}
satisfies
RC
and
(t_{1},t_{2})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{v_{2}}
, if
(t_{x}^{i-1},t_{2})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{v_{1}}
, we deduce that
(t_{1},t_{x}^{i-1})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
. However, as
\mathtt{round}(t_{x}^{i-1})<\mathtt{round}(t_{1})
,
(t_{x}^{i-1},t_{1})\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{t_{x}^{i-1}}
; which is impossible as
\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
. Thus, we conclude that
w_{v_{2}}^{t_{2}}=t_{1}
; which implies (2).

For proving (1) we observe that for every key
x\neq v_{0},v_{1},v_{2}
,
t_{x}^{i}=t_{x}^{i-1}
and by induction hypothesis we conclude that
\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{x}^{i},x)=(x,\alpha(x))
. Moreover, as
(t_{0},t_{1})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
and
(t_{1},t_{2})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
,
t_{v_{0}}^{i}=t_{v_{2}}^{i}=t_{2}
and
t_{v_{1}}^{i}=t_{1}
. In addition, as (3) holds,
\alpha(v_{0})=1
and
\alpha(v_{1})=\alpha(v_{2})=0
. This allow us to conclude (1) also for the keys
v_{0},v_{1}
and
v_{2}
; so the inductive step is proven.

After proving (1), (2) and (3) we can conclude that
\varphi
is
1
-in-
3
satisfiable. For every round
i
, we observe that by (1)
\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{x}^{i},x)=(x,\alpha(x))
. Moreover, as (2) holds,
(t_{x}^{i},t_{0}^{i})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}
; where
t_{0}^{i}
is the first transaction in
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
among the transactions in round
i
. As
\overline{h}
is a witness of
h_{\varphi}
,
\mathtt{WHERE}(r_{0}^{i})(\mathtt{value}_{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}}(t_{x}^{i},x))=0
; where
r_{0}^{i}
is the read event of
t_{0}^{i}
. Hence, exactly one variable among
v_{i}^{0},v_{i}^{1}
and
v_{i}^{2}
has
1
as image by
\alpha
. Therefore,
\varphi
is
1
-in-
3
satisfiable.

∎

0.B.3
Proof of
Theorem

4.3

See
4.3

The structure of the proof is divided in two parts: proving that the problem is NP and proving that it is NP-hard. The fist part, corresponding to
Lemma

18
, is straightforward as, for any client history, we simply guess a suitable witness and a total order on its transactions for deducing its consistency applying
Definition

6
. The second part, corresponding to Lemmas
24
and
25
is more complicated. We use a novel reduction from
3
-SAT. We encode a boolean formula
\varphi
in a history
h_{\varphi}
, s.t.
h_{\varphi}
is consistent iff
\varphi
is satisfiable. We first prove that the problem is indeed in NP (
Lemma

18
).

Lemma 18

The problem of checking consistency for a client history with an isolation configuration stronger than
RC
is in NP.

Proof

Let
h={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
a client history whose isolation configuration is stronger than
RC
. Guessing a witness of
h
,
\overline{h}
, and an execution of
\overline{h}
,
\xi={({\overline{h},{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}})}
, can be done in
\mathcal{O}(|\mathsf{Keys}|\cdot|\mathsf{events}({h})|^{2}+|T|^{2})\subseteq\mathcal{O}(|h|^{3})
. By
Lemma

5
, checking if
\xi
is consistent is equivalent as checking if
\textup{{saturate}}(\overline{h},{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}})
is an acyclic relation. As by
Lemma

7
,
Algorithm

1
requires polynomial time, we conclude the result.

∎

For showing NP-hardness, we reduce 3-SAT to the problem of checking consistency of a partial observation history. Note that the problem is NP-hard in the case where the isolation configuration is not saturable, as discussed in
Section

4.2
, using the results in
[
6
]
. Therefore, we only prove it for the case where the isolation configuration is saturable.

Let
\varphi=\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}C_{i}
a CNF expression with at most 3 literals per clause (i.e.
C_{i}=l_{i}^{1}\lor l_{i}^{2}\lor l_{i}^{3}
). Without loss of generality we can assume that each clause contains exactly three literals and each literal in a clause refers to a different variable. We denote
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})
to the variable that the literal
l_{i}^{j}
employs and
\mathsf{Vars}(\varphi)
the set of all variables of
\varphi
.

We design a history
h_{\varphi}
with an arbitrary saturable isolation configuration encoding
\varphi
. Thus, checking
\varphi
-satisfiability would reduce to checking
h_{\varphi}
’s consistency. Note that as
\mathsf{iso}(h_{\varphi})
is saturable,
h_{\varphi}
’s consistency is equivalent to checking
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}
’s acyclicity; where
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}=\textup{{saturate}}(h_{\varphi},({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}})^{+})
. We use the latter characterization of consistency for encoding the formula
\varphi
in
h_{\varphi}
.

\mathtt{INSERT}({\{\mathtt{var}(l_{i})_{(i,1)}^{+}:1\}})

…

\mathtt{INSERT}({\{\mathtt{var}(l_{i})_{(i,i-1)}^{+}:1\}})

\mathtt{INSERT}({\{\mathtt{var}(l_{i})_{(i,i+1)}^{+}:1\}})

…

\mathtt{INSERT}({\{\mathtt{var}(l_{i})_{(i,n)}^{+}:1\}})

\mathtt{INSERT}({\{\mathtt{var}(l_{i})_{(1,i)}^{-}:1\}})

…

\mathtt{INSERT}({\{\mathtt{var}(l_{i})_{(i-1,i)}^{-}:1\}})

\mathtt{INSERT}({\{\mathtt{var}(l_{i})_{(i+1,i)}^{-}:1\}})

…

\mathtt{INSERT}({\{\mathtt{var}(l_{i})_{(n,i)}^{-}:1\}})

\mathtt{INSERT}({\{\mathtt{var}(c_{i}^{j}):1\}})

\mathtt{DELETE}({\lambda r:\mathsf{key}(r)=\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}})

(a)

Transaction
t_{i}^{j}

\mathtt{INSERT}({\{\mathtt{var}(l_{i})_{(i,1)}^{-}:1\}})

…

\mathtt{INSERT}({\{\mathtt{var}(l_{i})_{(i,i-1)}^{-}:1\}})

\mathtt{INSERT}({\{\mathtt{var}(l_{i})_{(i,i+1)}^{-}:1\}})

…

\mathtt{INSERT}({\{\mathtt{var}(l_{i})_{(i,n)}^{-}:1\}})

\mathtt{INSERT}({\{\mathtt{var}(l_{i})_{(1,i)}^{+}:1\}})

…

\mathtt{INSERT}({\{\mathtt{var}(l_{i})_{(i-1,i)}^{+}:1\}})

\mathtt{INSERT}({\{\mathtt{var}(l_{i})_{(i+1,i)}^{+}:1\}})

…

\mathtt{INSERT}({\{\mathtt{var}(l_{i})_{(n,i)}^{+}:1\}})

\mathtt{INSERT}({\{\mathtt{var}(c_{i}^{j}):1\}})

\mathtt{INSERT}({\{\mathtt{var}(c_{i}^{j-1\bmod 3}):1\}})

\mathtt{DELETE}({\lambda r:\mathsf{key}(r)=\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}})

(b)

Transaction
\lnot t_{i}^{j}

\mathtt{INSERT}({\{x\in\mathtt{A}_{i}^{j}:0\}})

\mathtt{SELECT}(\lambda r:\mathsf{true})

(c)

Transaction
S_{i}^{j}

Figure 0.B.2
:
Description in full detail of the transactions
t_{i}^{j}
,
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
and
S_{i}^{j}
described in the proof of theorem
4.3
assuming
\mathtt{sign}(t_{i}^{j})=+
; where
\mathtt{A}_{i}^{j}
is the set of auxiliary variables for
S_{i}^{j}
. The case where
\mathtt{sign}(t_{i}^{j})=-
is analogous replacing in the first two instructions of both
t_{i}^{j}
and
\lnot t_{i}^{j}

+
by
-
and vice versa.

First of all, we consider every literal in
\varphi
independently. This means that even if two literals
l_{i}^{j}
and
l_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
share its variable (
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})=\mathtt{var}(l_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}})
) we will reason independently about them. For that, we employ keys
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}
and
\mathtt{var}(l_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}}
. We later enforce additional constraints for ensuring that
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}
and
\mathtt{var}(l_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}}
coordinate so assignments on
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}
coincide with assignments in
\mathtt{var}(l_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}}
. For simplicity in the explanation, whenever we talk about a literal
l
that is negated (for example
l\coloneqq\lnot x
), we denote by
\lnot l
to the literal
x
. Also, we use indistinguishably
x
when referring to a variable in
\varphi
or to a homonymous key in
h_{\varphi}
. In addition, with the aim of simplifying the explanation, we assume hereinafter that any occurrence of indices
i,i^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}
satisfy that
1\leq i,i^{\prime}\leq n
and
1\leq j,j^{\prime}\leq 3
.

For every clause
C_{i}=l_{i}^{1}\lor l_{i}^{2}\lor l_{i}^{3}
, we create nine transactions denoted by
t_{i}^{j}
,
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
and
S_{i}^{j}
. Figure
0.B.2
shows in detail their definition, which we explain and justify during the following lines. The transaction
t_{i}^{j}
represents the state where
l_{i}^{j}
is satisfied while
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
represents the state where
l_{i}^{j}
is unsatisfied. Transaction
S_{i}^{j}
is in charge of selecting one of the two states. With this goal on mind, transactions
t_{i}^{j}
and
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
contain a
\mathtt{DELETE}
event that deletes the key
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}
while
S_{i}^{j}
contains a
\mathtt{SELECT}
event that does
not
read
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}
in
h_{\varphi}
.
By
Definition

9
, any witnesses
h^{\prime}
of
h_{\varphi}
must read
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}
from a transaction that deletes it. As
h_{\varphi}
contain only two transactions that deletes such key (
t_{i}^{j}
and
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
), we can interpret that if
S_{i}^{j}
reads
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}
from
t_{i}^{j}
in
h^{\prime}
, then
l_{i}^{j}
is satisfied in
\varphi
while otherwise it is not.

For simplifying notation, as transactions
t_{i}^{j},\lnot t_{i}^{j},S_{i}^{j}
only have one read event, we define write-read dependencies directly from transactions instead of their read events. We also denote by
\mathtt{var}(t_{i}^{j})
and
\mathtt{var}(\lnot t_{i}^{j})
to the variable
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})
, associating each transaction with the variable of its associated literal.

We divide the construction of the history
h_{\varphi}
in two main parts. In the first part, we relate transactions
t_{i}^{j},\lnot t_{i}^{j}
and
S_{i}^{j}
with the clause
C_{i}
, ensuring a satisfying valuation of clause
C_{i}
corresponds to a consistent history when restricted to its associated transactions. In the second part, we link transactions associated to different clauses (for example
t_{i}^{j}
with
t_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
,
i\neq i^{\prime}
), for ensuring that valuations are consistent between clauses (i.e. a variable is not assigned
1
in clause
C_{i}
and
0
in clause
C_{i^{\prime}}
).

One

-cycle appears when no literal is satisfied

Figure 0.B.3
:
Transformation of the clause
C_{i}=x_{i}\lor y_{i}\lor\lnot z_{i}
into part of the history. Solid
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
-edges in
h_{\varphi}
represent the constraints of the clause while dashed
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
-edges, belonging only to the witnesses of
h_{\varphi}
, reflect the literals satisfied.

For the first part of
h_{\varphi}
’s construction, we observe that “at least one literal among
l_{i}^{1}
,
l_{i}^{2}
or
l_{i}^{3}
must be satisfied” is equivalent to “
\lnot l_{i}^{1}
,
\lnot l_{i}^{2}
and
\lnot l_{i}^{3}
cannot be satisfied at the same time”.
Thus, we add write-read dependencies to the history in such a way that if the three values that do not satisfy the clause are read by a witness
h^{\prime}
of
h_{\varphi}
, axiom
Read Committed
forces
h^{\prime}
to be inconsistent. We use an auxiliary key
c_{i}^{j}
written by transactions
t_{i}^{j}
,
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
and
\lnot t_{i}^{(j+1)\bmod 3}
and read by transaction
S_{i}^{j}
; enforcing
(\lnot t_{i}^{(j+1)\bmod 3},S_{i}^{j})\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{c_{i}^{j}}
. Thanks to variable
c_{i}^{j}
, if
(\lnot t_{i}^{j},S_{i}^{j})\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}}
in such witness
h^{\prime}
, for any consistent execution of
h^{\prime}
with commit order
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
,
(\lnot t_{i}^{j},\lnot t_{i}^{j+1\bmod 3})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
. Hence, if
h^{\prime}
is consistent, for every
i
there must exist a
j
s.t.
(t_{i}^{j},S_{i}^{j})\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}}
. Otherwise, every commit order witnessing
h^{\prime}
’s consistency would be cyclic; which is a contradiction.

In
Figure

0.B.3
we see how such
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
-cycle arise on any commit order witnessing
h_{\varphi}
’s consistency; where
\varphi
contains the clause
C_{i}=x_{i}\lor y_{i}\lor\lnot z_{i}
.

\mathtt{sign}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}+&\text{if }t=t_{i}^{j}\land l_{i}^{j}=\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})\\
-&\text{if }t=t_{i}^{j}\land l_{i}^{j}=\lnot\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})\\
-&\text{if }t=\lnot t_{i}^{j}\land l_{i}^{j}=\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})\\
+&\text{if }t=\lnot t_{i}^{j}\land l_{i}^{j}=\lnot\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})\\
\bot&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.\mathtt{opsign}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}+&\text{if }\mathtt{sign}(t)=-\\
-&\text{if }\mathtt{sign}(t)=+\\
\bot&\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(13)

For the second part of
h_{\varphi}
’s construction, we utilize the functions
\mathtt{sign}
and
\mathtt{opsign}
described in Equation
13
. The function
\mathtt{sign}
describes when a literal
l_{i}^{j}
is
positive
(i.e.
l_{i}^{j}=\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})
) or
negative
(i.e.
l_{i}^{j}=\lnot\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})
). If
l_{i}^{j}
is positive, it assigns to transaction
t_{i}^{j}
the symbol
+
and to
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
the symbol
-
; while if
l_{i}^{j}
is negative, the opposite. Such symbol is denoted the
sign
of a transaction.
Hence, for each transaction
t_{i}
s.t.
\mathtt{sign}(t_{i})\neq\bot
(i.e.
t_{i}
is either
t_{i}^{j}
or
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
), we introduce
n-1

\mathtt{INSERT}
events, one per key
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{(i,i^{\prime})}^{\mathtt{sign}(t_{i})}
,
i^{\prime}\neq i
, that write on that exact key.
Those
\mathtt{INSERT}
events are read by transaction
S_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
(i.e.
(t_{i},S_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}})\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}
, where
x=\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{(i,i^{\prime})}^{\mathtt{sign}(t_{i})}
). In addition, we introduce in
t_{i}
another
n-1

\mathtt{INSERT}
events that writes the key
\mathtt{var}(t_{i})_{(i^{\prime},i)}^{\mathtt{opsign}(t_{i})}
. Figure
0.B.2
describe the full description of transactions
t_{i}^{j}
and
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
.

:

x=\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})
. Superindices
j
are omitted for legibility. For simplicity on the Figure, we assume that
\mathtt{sign}(t_{k})=+
and
\mathtt{sign}(\lnot t_{k})=-
; the situation generalizes for any other setting. If
S_{i}^{j}
would read
x_{i}
from
t_{i}
in a witness
h^{\prime}
of
h_{\varphi}
(respectively
\lnot t_{i}
), for every
i^{\prime}\neq i

(t_{i},\lnot t_{i^{\prime}})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
, (resp.
(\lnot t_{i},t_{i^{\prime}})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
).
