{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}

The auxiliary keys
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{(i,i^{\prime})}^{\mathtt{sign}(t_{i})}
and
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{(i,i^{\prime})}^{\mathtt{opsign}(t_{i})}
are key to ensure consistency between clauses. Intuitively, if
S_{i}^{j}
reads
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}
from a positive transaction
t
in a consistent execution of
h_{\varphi}
,
\xi={({\overline{h},{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}})}
, and
t^{\prime}
is a negative transaction s.t.
\mathtt{var}(t)=\mathtt{var}(t^{\prime})
, then
(t,t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
; where
\overline{h}
is a witness of
h_{\varphi}
. Hence, any other transaction
S_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
must read
\mathtt{var}(l_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}}
also from a positive transaction in
\overline{h}
; otherwise
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
would be cyclic, which is impossible as
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
must be a total order. This phenomenon, that is depicted in Figure
0.B.4
, ensures that
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})
is always read from transactions with the same sign. In conclusion, we can establish consistent valuation of the variables of
\varphi
based on the write-read dependencies of the witnesses of
h_{\varphi}
.

We introduce a succint final part on the construction of
h_{\varphi}
for technical reasons. Indeed, any witness of
h_{\varphi}
ensures that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}^{-1}_{x}
is a total function for any
x\in\mathsf{Keys}
. We impose a few additional constraints on
h_{\varphi}
so we can better characterize the witnesses of
h_{\varphi}
. First, we assume that there exists an initial transaction that inserts, for every key
x
, a dummy value different from
\dagger_{x}
(for example
0
). Then, we impose that
t_{i}^{j}
and
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
read every key
x
from the initial transaction. Finally, for the case of transactions
S_{i}^{j}
, we define the set of
auxiliary keys

\mathtt{A}_{i}^{j}
that contain every key different from
c_{i}^{j},\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i},\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{(i^{\prime},i)}^{+}
and
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{(i^{\prime},i)}^{-}
. We introduce on
S_{i}^{j}
an
\mathtt{INSERT}
event that writes every key in
\mathtt{A}_{i}^{j}
with an abritrary value (for example,
0
). Hence,
S_{i}^{j}
reads every key in
A_{i}^{j}
from its own insert and no extra write-read dependency is required.

With this technical addendum, we define
h_{\varphi}={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
as the conjunction of all transactions and relations described above. In such case, the only information missing in
h_{\varphi}
to be a full history is
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}}^{-1}(S_{i}^{j})
. We assume that no more variables than the ones aforementioned belong to
\mathsf{Keys}
.

The proof of NP-hardness goes as follows: first, we prove in
Lemma

19
that
h_{\varphi}
is indeed a polynomial transformation of
\varphi
. Then, as
\mathsf{iso}(h_{\varphi})
is saturable, by Theorem
4.1
we observe that it suffices to prove that
\varphi
is satisfiable if and only there is a witness
\overline{h}
of
h_{\varphi}
s.t. the relation
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}=\textup{{saturate}}(\overline{h},({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+})
is acyclic. For simplifying the reasoning when
\mathsf{iso}(h_{\varphi})
has an arbitrary isolation configuration, we rely on
Lemma

20
for reducing the proof at the case when
\mathsf{iso}(h_{\varphi})=\textup{{RC}}
.

Hence, we prove on
Lemma

24
that on one hand, whenever
\varphi
is satisfiable we can construct a witness
\overline{h}
of
h_{\varphi}
based on such assignment for which
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}
is acyclic. For that, we require
Lemmas

21
,
22
and
23
.
On the other hand, whenever there is a consistent witness
\overline{h}
of
h_{\varphi}
, we prove on
Lemma

25
that we can construct a satisfying assignment of
\varphi
based on the write-read dependencies in
\overline{h}
. In this case, we require once more
Lemma

21
.

Lemma 19

The history
h_{\varphi}
has polynomial size on the length of
\varphi
.

Proof

Let
\varphi
a CNF with
n
clauses and
3
literals per clause. Then, as
\varphi
has
3n
literals,
h_{\varphi}
employs
9n
transactions plus one additional one (
init
). The number of keys,
|\mathsf{Keys}|
, is quadratic on
n
as transactions
t_{i}^{j}
and
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
insert
\mathcal{O}(n)
keys while
S_{i}^{j}
only insert keys also inserted by other transactions. Moreover, the number of events in
h_{\varphi}
,
\mathsf{events}({h_{\varphi}})
is in
\mathcal{O}(|\mathsf{Keys}|)=\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
as transactions
t_{i}^{j},\lnot t_{i}^{j}
have one
\mathtt{INSERT}
event per keys inserted (and they insert
\mathcal{O}(n)
keys) and one
\mathtt{DELETE}
event and transactions
S_{i}^{j}
only have two events. In addition,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\subseteq T\times T
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}\subseteq\mathsf{Keys}\times\mathsf{events}({h_{\varphi}})\times\mathsf{events}({h_{\varphi}})
; so their size is also polynomial on
n
. Thus,
h_{\varphi}
is a polynomial transformation of
\varphi
.

∎

One caveat of
h_{\varphi}
is that its isolation configuration is unknown.
Lemma

20
express that, in the particular case of
h_{\varphi}
, all saturable isolation levels stronger than
RC
are equivalent (they impose the same constraints). Hence, thereinafter we can assume without loss of generality that
\mathsf{iso}(h_{\varphi})=\textup{{RC}}
.

Lemma 20

Under history
h_{\varphi}
,
\mathsf{iso}(h_{\varphi})
is equivalent to
RC
(i.e.
\mathsf{iso}(h_{\varphi})
is both weaker and stronger than
RC
.)

Proof

Let
\overline{h}={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}})}
be any witness of
h_{\varphi}
and let
h^{\textup{{RC}}}
be the history that only differ with
\overline{h}
on its isolation configuration (
\mathsf{iso}(h^{\textup{{RC}}})=\textup{{RC}}
instead of
\mathsf{iso}(h)
). We prove that
\overline{h}
and
h^{\textup{{RC}}}
are simultaneously consistent or inconsistent.

As both
\mathsf{iso}(h_{\varphi})
and
RC
are saturable, by Theorem
4.1
, the proof is equivalent to prove that
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\textup{{RC}}}
are simultaneously cyclic or acyclic; where
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}=\textup{{saturate}}(\overline{h},({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\textup{{RC}}}=\textup{{saturate}}(h^{\textup{{RC}}},({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+})
. We prove that the two relations coincide, which allow us to conclude the result.

We observe that as
\mathsf{iso}(\overline{h})
is weaker than
RC
,
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\textup{{RC}}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}
. Thus, it suffices to prove that
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\textup{{RC}}}
. Let
t,t^{\prime}
be two transactions s.t.
(t,t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}
and let us prove that
(t,t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\textup{{RC}}}
. As
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\textup{{RC}}}
; we can assume without loss of generality that
(t,t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
. In such case, there exists
r\in\mathsf{reads}({\overline{h}}),x\in\mathsf{Keys}
and
v\in\mathsf{vis}(\mathsf{iso}(\overline{h})(\mathsf{tr}(r)))
s.t.
t^{\prime}={\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}}^{-1}(r)
and
\mathsf{v}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}})(t,r,x)
holds in
\overline{h}
. As
\mathsf{iso}(\overline{h})
is saturable,
(t,r)\in({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
.

First, we note that
\mathsf{tr}(r)\neq\textup{{{init}}}
as it does not contain any read event. As
t^{\prime}
is a
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
-predecessor of
\mathsf{tr}(r)
, and transactions
S_{i}^{j}
are
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
-maximal,
t^{\prime}
is not a
S_{i}^{j}
transaction; so it must be a
t_{i}^{j}
transaction. However, note that by construction of transactions
t_{i}^{j}
, their only
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
-predecessor is
init
. Thus, their only
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
-succesors can be transactions
S_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
; transactions that do not have
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
-successors. In conclusion, if
(t^{\prime},\mathsf{tr}(r))\in({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
,
(t^{\prime},\mathsf{tr}(r))\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}
, and therefore,
(t^{\prime},r)\in({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}});{\color[rgb]{0.7695,0.1172,0.2266}\mathsf{po}}^{*}
.

∎

Lemma

21
states a characterization of all commit order cycles imposed by the axiom
RC
that only relate the nine transactions associated to a clause in
\varphi
.

Lemma 21

Let
\overline{h}={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}})}
a witness of
h_{\varphi}
. For a fixed
i
, there is a
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}
-cycle relating
init
,
t_{i}^{j},\lnot t_{i}^{j}
and
S_{i}^{j}
,
1\leq j\leq 3
in
h
if and only if for all
1\leq j\leq 3
,
(\lnot t_{i}^{j},S_{i}^{j})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})}
in
\overline{h}
.

Proof

A graphical description of the different cases of this proof can be seen in Figure
0.B.3
.

\impliedby

Let us suppose that for every
j
,
1\leq j\leq 3
,
(\lnot t_{i}^{j},S_{i}^{j})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}}
. As
{\lnot t_{i}^{j}}\ \mathsf{writes}\ {c_{i}^{j}}
and
(\lnot t_{i}^{(j+1)\bmod 3},S_{i}^{j})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{c_{i}^{j}}
, by axiom
RC
we deduce that,
(\lnot t_{i}^{j},\lnot t_{i}^{(j+1)\bmod 3})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}
. Therefore, there is a
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}
-cycle between transactions
\lnot t_{i}^{1},\lnot t_{i}^{2}
and
\lnot t_{i}^{3}
.

\implies

First, note that
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}
is acyclic, so any
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}
-cycle has to include at least one
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
-dependency. Hence, let
t,t^{\prime}
be distinct transactions such that
(t^{\prime},t)\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
is an edge belonging to such cycle. By axiom
Read Committed
, this implies that there exists a read event
r
and a key
x
s.t.
(t,r)\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}
and
(t,r)\in({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}});{\color[rgb]{0.7695,0.1172,0.2266}\mathsf{po}}^{*}
. Note that in particular this means that
t^{\prime}
and
t
are two distinct
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
-succesors of
\mathsf{tr}(r)
.

We observe that
\mathsf{tr}(r)\neq\textup{{{init}}}
as
init
does not contain any read event. Moreover,
\mathsf{tr}(r)\neq t_{i}^{j},\lnot t_{i}^{j}
as those transactions have only one
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
-predecessor,
init
. Hence, there exists
j
s.t.
\mathsf{tr}(r)=S_{i}^{j}
. In this case, every ke written by
t_{i}^{j}
or
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
besides
c_{i}^{j}
and
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}
is read by
S_{i}^{j}
from the
\mathtt{INSERT}
event in its own transaction. We distinguish between two cases:

•

x=\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}
: The only transactions that write
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}
are
t_{i}^{j}
,
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
,
init
and transactions
S_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
. However, transactions
S_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
have only one
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
-succesor,
init
in
h_{\varphi}
. As
\forall x\neq\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i},{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}}^{-1}(S_{i}^{j})={{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}}^{-1}(S_{i}^{j})
, one of them,
init
must be either
t
or
t^{\prime}
. However,
t\neq\textup{{{init}}}
as
init
does not delete
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}
; so
t=\textup{{{init}}}
. But in such case,
(t^{\prime},t)\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
; which contradicts that
(t^{\prime},t)\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
. This proves that this case is impossible.

•

x=c_{i}^{j}
: In such case, as
(\lnot t_{i}^{j+1\bmod 3},S_{i}^{j})\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{c_{i}^{j}}
,
t=\lnot t_{i}^{j+1\bmod 3}
. The only transactions that writes
c_{i}^{j}
and are
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
-predecessors of
S_{i}^{j}
are
\textup{{{init}}},t_{i}^{j}
and
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
. As
(\textup{{{init}}},t)\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
;
t\neq\textup{{{init}}}
. Thus, any of the other two transactions are candidates to be
t^{\prime}
. Note that
(t^{\prime},t)\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}
is part of a cycle; so let
t^{\prime\prime}
be a transaction s.t.
(t^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}
.

If
(t^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime})\in({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
would hold, as for every key
x
,
{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}}^{-1}(t)={{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}}^{-1}(t)
,
t^{\prime\prime}=\textup{{{init}}}
. As
(t^{\prime},t)
is part of a
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}
cycle and
t\neq\textup{{{init}}}
, there must exist a transaction
t^{\prime\prime\prime}\neq t^{\prime\prime}=\textup{{{init}}}
s.t.
(t^{\prime\prime\prime},t^{\prime\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}
is part of such cycle. Note that
(t^{\prime\prime\prime},\textup{{{init}}})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
by construction of
h_{\varphi}
. Hence, there exists a key
y
and a read event
r^{\prime}
s.t.
(\textup{{{init}}},r^{\prime})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{y}
and
(t^{\prime\prime\prime},r^{\prime})\in({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}});{\color[rgb]{0.7695,0.1172,0.2266}\mathsf{po}}^{*}
. By construction of
h_{\varphi}
, if
(\textup{{{init}}},r^{\prime})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{y}
then
\mathsf{tr}(r^{\prime})
must be
t_{i}^{j^{\prime}}
for some
j^{\prime}
. But as we mentioned earlier, such transactions only have one
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
-predecessor,
init
; so it is impossible that
(t^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime})\in({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
.

Hence,
(t^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
. Replicating the same argument as before we can deduce that there exists a
j^{\prime}
s.t.
(t^{\prime\prime},S_{i}^{j^{\prime}})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{c_{i}^{j^{\prime}}}
,
t^{\prime}=\lnot t_{i}^{j^{\prime}+1\bmod 3}
and
t^{\prime\prime}
is either
t_{i}^{j^{\prime}}
or
\lnot t_{i}^{j^{\prime}}
. However, as discussed before,
t^{\prime}
could only be
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
or
t_{i}^{j}
. Therefore,
t^{\prime}=\lnot t_{i}^{j}
and
j=j^{\prime}+1\bmod 3
.

Finally, as
t^{\prime\prime}\neq t
, there must exist a transaction
t^{\prime\prime\prime}
s.t.
(t^{\prime\prime\prime},t^{\prime\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}
. By the same argument once more, there exists an index
j^{\prime\prime}
s.t.
t^{\prime\prime}=\lnot t_{i}^{j^{\prime\prime}+1\bmod 3}
,
(t^{\prime\prime\prime},S_{i}^{j^{\prime\prime}})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{c_{i}^{j^{\prime\prime}}}
and
t^{\prime\prime\prime}
is either
t_{i}^{j^{\prime\prime}}
or
\lnot t_{i}^{j^{\prime\prime}}
. Once more, as
t^{\prime\prime}
could only be
\lnot t_{i}^{j^{\prime}}
or
t_{i}^{j^{\prime}}
, we deduce that
j^{\prime}=j^{\prime\prime}+1\bmod 3
and
t^{\prime\prime}=\lnot t_{i}^{j^{\prime}}
. Note that in this case
j=j^{\prime\prime}+2\bmod 3
. Thus,
t=\lnot t_{i}^{j+1\bmod 3}=\lnot t_{i}^{j^{\prime\prime}}=t^{\prime\prime\prime}
. In conclusion, if such cycle exists it contain exactly the transactions
\lnot t_{i}^{1},\lnot t_{i}^{2}
and
\lnot t_{i}^{3}
and for each of them,
(\lnot t_{i}^{j},S_{i}^{j})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}}
.

∎

Lemma

22
states that any
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}
-dependencies imposed by the axiom
RC
on transactions
t,t^{\prime}
associated to diferent clauses in
\varphi
are related to valuation choices of literals in
\varphi
.

Lemma 22

Let
\overline{h}={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}})}
a witness of the history
h_{\varphi}
.
For every pair of transactions
t,t^{\prime}
and indices
i,j
, if
\mathtt{var}(t)=\mathtt{var}(t^{\prime})
,
{t^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{deletes}\ {\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}}
,
t\neq\lnot t_{i}^{j+1\bmod 3}
and
(t^{\prime},t)\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
in
h
, then
(t^{\prime},S_{i}^{j})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}}
.

Proof

Let
i,j
be indices and
t,t^{\prime}
be distinct transactions such that
t\neq\lnot t_{i}^{j+1\bmod 3}
and
(t^{\prime},t)\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
. Hence,
(t^{\prime},t)\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
, by axiom
Read Committed
, there must exist a key
x
and a read event
r
s.t.
(t,r)\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}
,
{t^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{writes}\ {x}
and
(t^{\prime},r)\in({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}});{\color[rgb]{0.7695,0.1172,0.2266}\mathsf{po}}^{*}
. We characterize the possible candidates of transactions
t,t^{\prime},\mathsf{tr}(r)
and key
x
.

First, as
\mathsf{tr}(r)
has two different
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
-predecessors,
\mathsf{tr}(r)\neq\textup{{{init}}},t_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
; for any indices
i^{\prime},j^{\prime}
. Hence, there must exist indices
i^{\prime},j^{\prime}
s.t.
\mathsf{tr}(r)=S_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
.

Next we deduce that
t
and
t^{\prime}
belongs to different clauses. As
{t^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{deletes}\ {\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}}
, we deduce that
t^{\prime}
is either
t_{i}^{j}
or
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
. Hence, as
t\neq\lnot t_{i}^{j+1\bmod 3}
, neither
t_{i}^{j}
nor
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
are
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
-predecessors of
S_{i}^{j}
but both
t
and
t^{\prime}
are
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
-predecessors of
S_{i}^{j}
, we then deduce that
t
and
t^{\prime}
belong to different clauses.

Finally, we deduce that
i^{\prime}=i
and
(t^{\prime},S_{i}^{j})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}}
. As
x
is written by
t
and
t^{\prime}
and
x\not\in\mathtt{A}_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
; either
t
or
t^{\prime}
are associated to the same clause as
S_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
. If
t
would be associated to clause
C_{i^{\prime}}
, then
t
should be either
t_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
or
\lnot t_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
and
x=\mathtt{var}(l_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}}
. However, this contradicts that
t^{\prime}
writes
\mathtt{var}(l_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}})_{i^{\prime}}
as
t^{\prime}
is either
t_{i}^{j}
or
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
. Hence, as
t
is not associated to clause
C_{i^{\prime}}
,
i^{\prime}=i
. As
(t^{\prime},S_{i}^{j})\not\in({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}})
but
(t^{\prime},S_{i}^{j})\in({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
and
\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{y}={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{y}
for any key
y\neq\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}
, we conclude that
(t^{\prime},S_{i}^{j})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}}
.

∎

Lemma

23
states that
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}
does not contain tuples of transactions associated to literals with equal variable and sign.

Lemma 23

Let
\overline{h}={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}})}
a witness of the history
h_{\varphi}
. For every pair of transactions
t,t^{\prime}
and indices
i,j
, if
\mathtt{sign}(t)=\mathtt{sign}(t^{\prime})
,
\mathtt{var}(t)=\mathtt{var}(t^{\prime})=\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})
,
(t,S_{i}^{j})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}}
and
t^{\prime}\neq\lnot t_{i}^{j-1\bmod 3}
then
(t^{\prime},t)\not\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
.

Proof

We reason by contradiction. Let us suppose that
t,t^{\prime}
are a pair of transactions such that
\mathtt{sign}(t)=\mathtt{sign}(t^{\prime})
,
\mathtt{var}(t)=\mathtt{var}(t^{\prime})=\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})
,
(t,S_{i}^{j})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(t)_{i}}
,
t^{\prime}\neq\lnot t^{j-1\bmod 3}
and
(t^{\prime},t)\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
, for some indices
i,j
. As
(t,S_{i}^{j})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}}
,
t
is either
t_{i}^{j}
or
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
. Moreover, as
(t^{\prime},t)\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
, by axiom
Read Committed
we deduce that there exists a key
x
and a read event
r
s.t.
(t^{\prime},r)\in({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}});{\color[rgb]{0.7695,0.1172,0.2266}\mathsf{po}}^{*}
,
(t,r)\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}
and
{t^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{writes}\ {x}
.

We first prove that
t^{\prime}
and
t
are associated to different clauses. As
(\textup{{{init}}},t)\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
,
t^{\prime}\neq\textup{{{init}}}
. Next, as
(t^{\prime},r)\in({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}});{\color[rgb]{0.7695,0.1172,0.2266}\mathsf{po}}^{*}
and transactions
S_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
are
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}
-maximal, we deduce that there must exist a pair of indices
i^{\prime},j^{\prime}
s.t.
t^{\prime}=t_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
or
\lnot t_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
. Moreover, as
t^{\prime}\neq\lnot t_{i}^{j-1\bmod 3}
,
t
is either
t_{i}^{j}
or
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
. In addition, as in any witness of
h_{\varphi}
both
t_{i}^{j}
and
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
cannot be
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
-predecessors of
S_{i}^{j}
, we deduce that
i^{\prime}\neq i
.

Finally we contradict the hypothesis proving that
\mathtt{sign}(t)\neq\mathtt{sign}(t^{\prime})
. If
i^{\prime}\neq i
,
t\neq\textup{{{init}}}
but
t
is a
\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}
-predecessor of
\mathsf{tr}(r)
, there must exist indices
i^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime}
s.t.
\mathsf{tr}(r)=S_{i^{\prime\prime}}^{j^{\prime\prime}}
. Hence, as
x\in\mathtt{A}_{i^{\prime\prime}}^{j^{\prime\prime}}
and it is written by
t
and
t^{\prime}
,
i^{\prime\prime}
must be either
i^{\prime}
or
i
. However,
i^{\prime\prime}\neq i
as in that case,
x=\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}
and
t^{\prime}
does not write
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}
. Hence,
i^{\prime\prime}=i^{\prime}\neq i
and
x=\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{(i^{\prime},i)}^{\mathtt{sign}(t^{\prime})}
. However, as
{t}\ \mathsf{writes}\ {x}
, by construction of
h_{\varphi}
, we must conclude that
\mathtt{sign}(t)\neq\mathtt{sign}(t^{\prime})
. Thus, as we reached a contradiction, the lemma holds.

∎

Lemma 24

For every boolean formula
\varphi
, if
\varphi
is satisfiable then there is a consistent witness
\overline{h}
of
h_{\varphi}
.

Proof

Let
\alpha:\mathsf{Vars}(\varphi)\to\{0,1\}
an assignment that satisfies
\varphi
. Let
h_{\varphi}^{\alpha}={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}})}
the extension of
h_{\varphi}
s.t. for every
i,j
,
(t_{i}^{j},S_{i}^{j})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}}
if
l_{i}^{j}[\alpha(\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j}))/\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})]=\mathsf{true}
and
(\lnot t_{i}^{j},S_{i}^{j})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}}
otherwise. Note that for every two transactions
t,t^{\prime}
s.t.
\mathtt{var}(t)=\mathtt{var}(t^{\prime})
,
\alpha(\mathtt{var}(t))=\alpha(\mathtt{var}(t^{\prime}))
. Hence, if
(t,S_{i}^{j})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(t)_{i}}
and
(t^{\prime},S_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(t^{\prime})_{i^{\prime}}}
then
\mathtt{sign}(t)=\mathtt{sign}(t^{\prime})
. In addition, by construction of
h_{\varphi}
, for every transaction
S_{i}^{j}
, the only variable
x
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}^{-1}(S_{i}^{j})\uparrow
is
x=\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})
.
Thus, for every
x\in\mathsf{Keys}
,
{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}}^{-1}
is defined for any read that does not read locally and therefore,
h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}
is a full history that extends
h_{\varphi}
.

Let us prove that
h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}
is consistent. As mentioned before, thanks to Theorem
4.1
, we can reduce the problem of checking if
h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}
is consistent to the problem of checking if
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}=\textup{{saturate}}(h^{\alpha}_{\varphi},({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+})
is acyclic.

We reason by contradiction, assuming there is a
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
-cycle and reaching a contradiction. Clearly
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}
is acyclic as
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
is acyclic, transactions
S_{i}^{j}
are
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
-maximal and
\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
only contains tuples
(t_{i}^{j},S_{i}^{j})
or
(\lnot t_{i}^{j},S_{i}^{j})
. Thus, any
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
-cycle in
h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}
contains at least one edge
(t^{\prime},t)\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
; so let be
t,t^{\prime}
such a pair of distinct transactions s.t.
(t^{\prime},t)\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
and
(t^{\prime},t)
is part of the
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
-cycle.

First, we observe that by construction of
h_{\varphi}
, transactions
S_{i}^{j}
are
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
-maximal. Moreover, they also
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
-maximal: by contradiction, if there was a transaction
u_{i}^{j}
s.t.
(S_{i}^{j},u_{i}^{j})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})+
, by axiom
Read Committed
, there would be a variable a read event
r
s.t.
(S_{i}^{j},r)\in({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}});{\color[rgb]{0.7695,0.1172,0.2266}\mathsf{po}}^{*}
; which is impossible. We also observe that
init
is not only
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
-minimal but
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
-minimal. By the same argument, if there would be a transaction
u\neq\textup{{{init}}}
s.t.
(u,\textup{{{init}}})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
, by axiom
Read Committed
, there should be a read event
r
and a key
x
s.t.
(\textup{{{init}}},r)\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}
and
(u^{\prime},r)\in({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}});{\color[rgb]{0.7695,0.1172,0.2266}\mathsf{po}}^{*}
. However, by construction of
h_{\varphi}
, the only transactions that read a variable from
init
are
t_{i}^{j}
; transactions with only one
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
-predecessor. This shows that such transaction
u
does not exist. Altogether, the
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
-cycle can only contain pairs of transactions
t_{i}^{j}
and
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
. In particular, as transactions
t_{i}^{j}
have only one
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
-predecessor,
init
, such
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
-cycle is in
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
.

Next, we note that, as every clause
C_{i}
is satisfied by
\alpha
, there exists an index
j
s.t.
(t_{i}^{j},S_{i}^{j})\in\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})
. By
Lemma

21
, we know there is no
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
-cycle relating the nine transactions associated with clause
C_{i}
and
init
. Therefore, a
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
-cycle has to involve at least two transactions from different clauses. Hence, we can assume without loss of generality that
t
and
t^{\prime}
belong to the same clause.

As
(t^{\prime},t)\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
, there must exist a key
x
and a read event
r_{x}
s.t.
{t}\ \mathsf{writes}\ {x}
,
(t,r_{x})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}
and
(t^{\prime},r)\in({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}});{\color[rgb]{0.7695,0.1172,0.2266}\mathsf{po}}^{*}
.
By construction of
h_{\varphi}
, the only case when two transactions from different clauses write the same variable is when
\mathtt{var}(t)=\mathtt{var}(t^{\prime})
. In particular, as
t
and
t^{\prime}
belong to different clauses, there must exist indices
i,i^{\prime}
s.t.
x=\mathtt{var}(t)^{\mathtt{sign}(t)}_{(i^{\prime},i)}
and
\mathsf{tr}(r_{x})=S_{i}^{j}
. Hence, there is only one candidate for transaction
t^{\prime}
:
t_{i}^{j}
if
\mathtt{sign}(t_{i}^{j})=\mathtt{sign}(t^{\prime})=\mathtt{opsign}(t)
and
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
otherwise. Therefore, as
t^{\prime},\mathsf{tr}(r_{x})
belong to the same clause and
t^{\prime}
is a
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
-predecessor of
\mathsf{tr}(r_{x})
, we conclude that
(t^{\prime},S_{i}^{j})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(t^{\prime})_{i}}
.

To reach a contradiction, we find a pair of distinct transactions
\tilde{t},\hat{t}
in the
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
-cycle from different clauses but associated to the same variable.
First, as
(t^{\prime},t)
is part of the
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
-cycle, there exists a
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
-predecessor of
t^{\prime}
,
t^{\prime\prime}
s.t.
(t^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
is part of the
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
-cycle. As we mentioned before,
(t^{\prime\prime},t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
. Then, there must exist a key
y
and a read event
r_{y}
s.t.
{t^{\prime\prime}}\ \mathsf{writes}\ {y}
,
(t^{\prime},r_{y})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{y}
and
(t^{\prime\prime},r)\in({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}});{\color[rgb]{0.7695,0.1172,0.2266}\mathsf{po}}^{*}
. Two cases arise:

•

t^{\prime\prime}
is not associated to clause
i
: In this case, as both
t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}
write variable
y
, by construction of
h_{\varphi}
we observe that
\mathtt{var}(t^{\prime\prime})=\mathtt{var}(t^{\prime})
. Thus, we denote
\tilde{t}=t^{\prime\prime}
and
\hat{t}=t^{\prime}
.

•

t^{\prime\prime}
is associated to clause
i
: In this case,
t^{\prime\prime}\neq\lnot t^{\prime}
as no transaction in
h
have both
t^{\prime}
and
\lnot t^{\prime}
as
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})
-predecessors. Hence, as no clause has two literals referring to the same variable,
\mathtt{var}(t^{\prime})\neq\mathtt{var}(t^{\prime\prime})
. Thus, as
t^{\prime\prime}
and
t^{\prime}
have one common key, we deduce that
t^{\prime\prime}=\lnot t_{i}^{j-1\bmod 3}
and
y=c_{i}^{j-1\bmod 3}
. Thus, as
(t^{\prime},r)\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{c_{i}^{j-1\bmod 3}}
, we can conclude that
\mathsf{tr}(r_{y})=S_{i}^{j-1\bmod 3}
and
(t^{\prime\prime},S_{i}^{j-1\bmod 3})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(t^{\prime\prime})_{i}}
. As
t^{\prime\prime}\neq t
, there must exist a transaction
t^{\prime\prime\prime}
s.t.
(t^{\prime\prime\prime},t^{\prime\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
belongs to the
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
-cycle. Again, we observe two cases:

–

t^{\prime\prime\prime}
is not associated to clause
i
: In this case, by an analogous argument, we observe that
\mathtt{var}(t^{\prime\prime\prime})=\mathtt{var}(t^{\prime\prime})
. Thus, we denote
\tilde{t}=t^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
\hat{t}=t^{\prime\prime}
.

–

t^{\prime\prime\prime}
is associated to clause
i
: By the same reasoning as before,
t^{\prime\prime\prime}=\lnot t_{i}^{j-2\bmod 3}
and
(t^{\prime\prime\prime},S_{i}^{j-2\bmod 3})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(t^{\prime\prime\prime})_{i}}
. Moreover, as
t^{\prime\prime\prime}\neq t
, there must exist a transaction
t^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}
s.t.
(t^{\prime\prime\prime\prime},t^{\prime\prime\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
belongs to the
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
-cycle. Moreover,
t^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}
is not associated to clause
i
, as, once more, we would deduce that
t^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}=\lnot t_{i}^{j-3\bmod 3}
and that
(t^{\prime\prime\prime\prime},S_{i}^{j-3\bmod 3})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(t^{\prime\prime\prime\prime})_{i}}
; which is impossible as by the construction of
h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}
is satisfied. Hence,
t^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}
and
t^{\prime\prime\prime}
belong to different clauses and
\mathtt{var}(t^{\prime\prime\prime\prime})=\mathtt{var}(t^{\prime\prime\prime})
. We denote in this case
\tilde{t}=t^{\prime\prime\prime\prime}
and
\hat{t}=t^{\prime\prime\prime}
.

Finally, we reach a contradiction with the help of Lemmas
23
and
22
.
On one hand, by the choice of transactions
\hat{t}
and
\tilde{t}
, we know that
\mathtt{var}(\hat{t})=\mathtt{var}(\tilde{t})
and there exist indices
\tilde{i},\tilde{j}
s.t.
{\tilde{t}}\ \mathsf{deletes}\ {\mathtt{var}(l_{\tilde{i}}^{\tilde{j}})}
.
Moreover,
\hat{t}\neq\lnot\tilde{t}_{\tilde{i}}^{\tilde{j}+1\bmod 3}
as they belong to different clauses. Thus, as
(\tilde{t},\hat{t})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
, by
Lemma

22
we deduce that
(\tilde{t},S_{\tilde{i}}^{\tilde{j}})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(\tilde{t})_{i}}
.
On the other hand, we also know that there exist indices
\hat{i},\hat{j}
s.t.
\hat{t}
is associated to the literal
l_{\hat{i}}^{\hat{j}}
and
(\hat{t},S_{\hat{i}}^{\hat{j}})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(t)_{\hat{i}}}
.
Hence, by construction of
h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}
, as
\mathtt{var}(\hat{t})=\mathtt{var}(\tilde{t})
,
(\tilde{t},S_{\tilde{i}}^{\tilde{j}})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(\tilde{t})_{i}}
and
(\hat{t},_{\hat{i}}^{\hat{j}})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(\hat{t})_{\hat{i}}}
, we deduce that
\mathtt{sign}(\hat{t})=\mathtt{sign}(\tilde{t})
. However, by
Lemma

23
, we deduce that
(\tilde{t},\hat{t})\not\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
. This contradicts that
(\tilde{t},\hat{t}){h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
is part of the
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
-cycle. Thus, the initial hypothesis, that
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
is cyclic, is false. In conclusion,
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}}
is acyclic, so
h_{\varphi}
is consistent as
h^{\alpha}_{\varphi}
is a consistent witness of
h_{\varphi}
.

∎

Lemma 25

For every boolean formula
\varphi
, if there is a consistent witness of
h
, then
\varphi
is satisfiable.

Proof

Let
\overline{h}={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}})}
be a consistent witness of
h_{\varphi}
. Hence, by Theorem
4.1
, the relation
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}=\textup{{saturate}}(\overline{h},({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+})
is acyclic. We use this fact to construct a satisfying assignment of
\varphi
. Let us call
u_{i}^{j}
to the transaction s.t.
(u_{i}^{j},S_{i}^{j})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}}
. Note that by construction of
h_{\varphi}
,
{u_{i}^{j}}\ \mathsf{deletes}\ {\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})_{i}}
, so
u_{i}^{j}
is either
t_{i}^{j}
or
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
.

We first prove that for every pair of pairs of indices
i,i^{\prime},j,j^{\prime}
, if
\mathtt{var}(u_{i}^{j})=\mathtt{var}(u_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}})
then
\mathtt{sign}(u_{i}^{j})=\mathtt{sign}(u_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}})
. By contradiction, let
u_{i}^{j},u_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
be a pair of transactions s.t.
\mathtt{var}(u_{i}^{j})=\mathtt{var}(u_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}})
and
\mathtt{sign}(u_{i}^{j})\neq\mathtt{sign}(u_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}})
. In such case,
\mathtt{opsign}(u_{i}^{j})=\mathtt{sign}(u_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}})
. Thus, both transactions write
\mathtt{var}(u_{i}^{j})_{(i^{\prime},i)}^{\mathtt{opsign}(u_{i}^{j})}
and
\mathtt{var}(u_{i}^{j})_{(i,i^{\prime})}^{\mathtt{sign}(u_{i}^{j})}
. By axiom
Read Committed
, as
(u_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}},S_{i}^{j})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(u_{i}^{j})_{(i^{\prime},i)}^{\mathtt{opsign}(u_{i}^{j})}}
and
(u_{i}^{j},S_{i}^{j})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}
, we conclude that
(u_{i}^{j},u_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}
. By a symmetric argument using
\mathtt{var}(u_{i}^{j})_{(i,i^{\prime})}^{\mathtt{sign}(u_{i}^{j})}
we deduce that
(u_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}},u_{i}^{j})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}
. However, this is impossible as
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}
is acylclic; so we conclude that indeed
\mathtt{sign}(u_{i}^{j})=\mathtt{sign}(u_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}})
.

Next, we construct a map that assign at each variable in
\varphi
a value
0
or
1
. Let
\alpha_{h}:\mathsf{Vars}(\varphi)\to\{0,1\}
be the map that assigns for each variable
\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})
the value
1
if
\mathtt{sign}(u_{i}^{j})=+
and
0
if
\mathtt{sign}(u_{i}^{j})=-
. Note that this map is well defined as, by the previous paragraph, if two literals
l_{i}^{j},l_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
share variable, then their respective transactions
u_{i}^{j},u_{i^{\prime}}^{j^{\prime}}
have the same sign.

Finally, we prove that
\varphi
is satisfied with this assignment. By construction of
\alpha_{h}
, for every pair of indices
i,j
,
l_{i}^{j}[\alpha_{h}(\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j}))/\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})]
is
\mathsf{true}
if and only if
(t_{i}^{j},S_{i}^{j})\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{\mathtt{var}(l_{i}^{j})}
. Moreover, as
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}_{\overline{h}}
is acyclic, by
Lemma

21
, we know that for each
i
there exists a
j
s.t.
u_{i}^{j}\neq\lnot t_{i}^{j}
. Hence, for this
j
,
u_{i}^{j}
must be
t_{i}^{j}
as
u_{i}^{j}
is either
t_{i}^{j}
or
\lnot t_{i}^{j}
. Therefore, every clause is satisfied using
\alpha_{h}
as assignment; so
\varphi
is satisfiable.

∎

0.B.4
Proof of
Theorem

5.1
.

See
5.1

The proof of
Theorem

5.1
is a consequence of
Lemmas

27
and
30
.

Lemma 26

Let
h={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
be a client history,
P={({T_{P},M_{P}})}
be a consistent prefix of
h
and
t\in T\setminus T_{P}
. If
(P\cup\{t\})\in\mathtt{seen}
then
exploreConsistentPrefixes
(h,P\cup\{t\})
returns
\mathsf{false}
.

Proof

If
(P\cup\{t\})\in\mathtt{seen}
, then
P\cup\{t\}
has been to
\mathtt{seen}
added at line
6
of
Algorithm

4
. To execute such instruction, the condition at line
4
,
exploreConsistentPrefixes
({h},P\cup\{t\})
returns
\mathsf{true}
, does not hold; which let us conclude the result.

∎

Lemma 27

Let
h={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
be a client history whose isolation configuration is stronger than
RC
. If
h
is consistent, Algorithm
3
returns
\mathsf{true}
.

Proof

Let
h
be a consistent history that satisfies the hypothesis of the Lemma. As
h
is consistent, let
\overline{h}={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}})}
be a witness of
h
and let
\xi={({\overline{h},{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}})}
be a consistent execution of
\overline{h}
. We first reduce the problem to prove that
Algorithm

4
returns true on a particular witness of
h
, a history
\hat{h}
s.t.
h\subseteq\hat{h}\subseteq\overline{h}
.

First, let
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}},E_{h}
and
X_{h}
be defined as in
Algorithm

3
at lines
2
-
4
. As
\overline{h}
is consistent, for every
\mathtt{read}
event
r
and a variable
x
s.t.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}^{-1}(r)\uparrow
,
\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}^{-1}(r)\downarrow
and
\mathtt{WHERE}(r)(\mathtt{value}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}(t_{x}^{r},x))=0
; where
t_{x}^{r}=\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}^{-1}(r)
.

On one hand, if
E_{h}
is empty,
X_{h}
is empty as well. In such case, we denote
\hat{h}=h
. On the other hand, if
E_{h}\neq\emptyset
, for every
(r,x)\in E_{h}
we know that the transaction
t_{x}^{r}
belongs to
\mathtt{0}_{x}^{r}
. Therefore,
X_{h}\neq\emptyset
. Thus, let
f
be the map that assigns for every pair
(r,x)\in E_{h}
the transaction
t_{x}^{r}
; and let
\hat{h}={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}})}
be the history s.t.
\hat{h}=h\bigoplus_{(r,x)\in E_{h}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}(f(r,x),r)
. We observe that the fact that
\hat{h}
is a history and
\overline{h}
witnesses
\hat{h}
’s consistency using
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
is immediate as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}\subseteq\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}\subseteq\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}
.
Note that in both cases, the condition at line
6
does not hold. Therefore, to prove that
Algorithm

3
returns
\mathsf{true}
it suffices to prove that
\textup{{exploreConsistentPrefixes}}(\hat{h},\emptyset)
returns
\mathsf{true}
.

We define an inductive sequence of prefixes based on
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
and show that they represent recursive calls to
Algorithm

4
. As a base case, let
P_{0}
be the prefix with only
init
as transaction. Assuming that for every
j,0\leq j\leq i
,
P_{i}
is defined, let
P_{i+1}=P_{i}\cup\{t_{i}\}
; where
t_{i}
is the
i
-th transaction of
T
according to
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
. By construction of
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
. Hence, Property
1
immediately holds. Moreover, as
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
witnesses
\overline{h}
’s consistency, Property
2
also holds; so
P_{i}\triangleright_{t_{i+1}}P_{i+1}
.

We conclude showing by induction on the number of transactions that are not in the prefix that for every
i,0\leq i\leq|T|
,
exploreConsistentPrefixes
(\hat{h},P_{i})
returns
\mathsf{true}
.

•

Base case:
The base case is
i=|T|
. In such case,
P_{|T|}
contains all transactions in
T
. Therefore, the condition at line
2
in
Algorithm

4
holds and the algorithm returns
\mathsf{true}
.

•

Inductive case:
The inductive hypothesis guarantees that for every
k,i\leq k\leq|T|
,
exploreConsistentPrefixes
(\hat{h},P_{i})
returns
\mathsf{true}
and we show that
exploreConsistentPrefixes
(\hat{h},P_{i-1})
also returns
\mathsf{true}
. By definition of
P_{i}
,
T_{P_{i}}=T_{P_{i-1}}\cup\{t_{i}\}
. In particular,
|P_{i}|\neq|T|
and
P_{i-1}\triangleright_{t_{i+1}}P_{i}
. In addition, by induction hypothesis, we know that
exploreConsistentPrefixes
(\hat{h},P_{i})
returns
\mathsf{true}
. Hence, by
Lemma

26
,
P_{i}\not\in\mathtt{seen}
.
Altogether, we deduce that
exploreConsistentPrefixes
(\hat{h},P_{i-1})
returns
\mathsf{true}
.

∎

Lemma 28

Let
\hat{h}={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}})}
be a client history and
P={({T_{P},M_{P}})}
be a consistent prefix. If
exploreConsistentPrefixes
(\hat{h},P)
returns
\mathsf{true}
, there exist distinct transactions
t_{i}\in T\ T_{P}
and a collection of consistent prefixes
P_{i}={({T_{P},M_{P}})}
s.t.
P_{i}=P_{i-1}\cup\{t_{i}\}
,
P_{i-1}\triangleright_{t_{i}}P_{i}
and
exploreConsistentPrefixes
(\hat{h},P_{i})
returns
\mathsf{true}
; where
|T_{P}|<i\leq|T|
and
P_{|T_{P}|}=P
.

Proof

Let
\hat{h}
be a client history and
P={({T_{P},M_{P}})}
be a consistent prefix s.t.
exploreConsistentPrefixes
(\hat{h},P)
returns
\mathsf{true}
. We prove the result by induction on the number of transactions not present in
T_{P}
.
The base case, when
|T_{P}|=|T|
, immediately holds as
T\setminus T_{P}=\emptyset
. Let us assume that the inductive hypothesis holds for any prefix containing
k
transactions and let us show that it also holds for every consistent prefix with
k-1
transactions. Let us thus assume that
|T_{P}|=k-1
. As
exploreConsistentPrefixes
(\hat{h},P)
returns
\mathsf{true}
, it must reach line
5
in
Algorithm

4
. Hence, there must exist a transaction
t_{k}\in T\setminus T_{P}
s.t.
P\triangleright_{t_{k}}(P\cup\{t_{k}\})
and
exploreConsistentPrefixes
(\hat{h},P\cup\{t_{k}\})
returns
\mathsf{true}
. By induction hypothesis on
P\cup\{t_{k}\}={({T_{k},M_{k}})}
, there exist a distinct transactions
t_{i}\in T\setminus T_{k}
and a collection consistent prefixes
P_{i}
s.t.
P_{i}=P_{i-1}\cup\{t_{i}\}
,
P_{i-1}\triangleright_{t_{i}}P_{i}
and
exploreConsistentPrefixes
(\hat{h},P_{i})
returns
\mathsf{true}
; where
k<i\leq|T|
and
P_{k}=P\cup\{t_{k}\}
. Thus, the inductive step holds thanks to prefix
P_{k}
.

∎

Lemma 29

Let
h={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
be a client history and let
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}
be the relation defined as at line
2
in
Algorithm

4
. If
checkConsistency
(h)
returns
\mathsf{true}
, there exists an extension
\hat{h}={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}})}
of
h
s.t. for every
\mathtt{read}
event
r
, variable
x
and transaction
t
, (1) if
(t,r)\in\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}
then
t\in\mathtt{0}_{x}^{r}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}})
, (2) if
\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}^{-1}(r)\uparrow
then
\mathtt{1}_{x}^{r}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}})=\emptyset
, and (3)
exploreConsistentPrefixes
(\hat{h},\emptyset)
returns
\mathsf{true}
.

Proof

Let
h={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
be a client history s.t.
checkConsistency
(h)
returns
\mathsf{true}
and let
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}},E_{h},X_{h}
be the objects described in lines
2
-
4
in
Algorithm

3
. If there exists a pair
(r,x)\in E_{h}
for which
\mathtt{0}_{x}^{r}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}})=\emptyset
,
checkConsistency
(h)
returns
\mathsf{false}
. Hence,
E_{h}
is empty if and only if
X_{h}
is empty. If
E_{h}=\emptyset
,
Algorithm

3
executes line
7
. Thus, taking
\hat{h}=h
, conditions (1), (2) and (3) trivially hold. Otherwise,
Algorithm

3
executes line
8
. Once again, as
exploreConsistentPrefixes
(h,\emptyset)
returns
\mathsf{true}
, there must exists
f\in X_{h}
s.t.
exploreConsistentPrefixes
(\hat{h},\emptyset)
returns
\mathsf{true}
; where
\hat{h}=\bigoplus_{(r,x)\in E_{h}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}(f(r,x),r)
. Thanks to the definition of
f
and
\hat{h}
conditions (1), (2) and (3) are satisfied.

∎

Lemma 30

Let
h={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
be a client history whose isolation configuration is composed of
\{\textup{{SER}},\textup{{SI}},\textup{{PC}},\textup{{RC}}\}
isolation levels. If
Algorithm

4
returns
\mathsf{true}
,
h
is consistent.

Proof

Let
h={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
be a client history s.t.
checkConsistency
(h)
returns
\mathsf{true}
and let
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}},E_{h}
and
X_{h}
be defined as at lines
2
-
4
in
Algorithm

3
. By Lemma
29
, there exists an extension of
h

\hat{h}={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}})}
s.t. for every
\mathtt{read}
event
r
, variable
x
and transaction
t
, (1) if
\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}^{-1}(r)\uparrow
then
\mathtt{1}_{x}^{r}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}})=\emptyset
, (2) if
(t,r)\in\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}
then
t\in\mathtt{0}_{x}^{r}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}})
and (3)
exploreConsistentPrefixes
(\hat{h},\emptyset)
returns
\mathsf{true}
. By
Lemma

28
applied on
\hat{h}
and
\emptyset
, there exist distinct transactions
t_{i}\in T
and a collection of prefixes of
h
,
P_{i}={({T_{i},M_{i}})}
, s.t.
P_{i}=P_{i-1}\cup\{t_{i}\}
,
P_{i-1}\triangleright_{t_{i}}P_{i}
and
\textup{{exploreConsistentPrefixes}}(\hat{h},P_{i})
returns
\mathsf{true}
; where
P_{0}=\emptyset
and
0<i\leq|T|
. Let
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
be the total order based on the aforementioned transactions
t_{i}
, i.e.
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}=\{(t_{i},t_{j})\ |\ i<j\}
. We construct a full history that extends
\hat{h}
employing
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
and taking into account the isolation level of each transaction.

For every
\mathtt{read}
event
r
, key
x
and visibility relation
\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{vis}(\mathsf{iso}(h)(\mathsf{tr}(r)))
, let
t_{\mathsf{v}},t_{x}^{r}
be the transactions defined as follows:

\displaystyle t_{\mathsf{v}}^{x}=\max_{{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}}\{t^{\prime}\in T\ |\ {t^{\prime}}\ \mathsf{writes}\ {x}\ \land\ \mathsf{v}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}})(t^{\prime},r,x)\}

\displaystyle t_{x}^{r}=\max_{{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}}\{t_{\mathsf{v}}^{x}\ |\ \mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{vis}(\mathsf{iso}(h)(\mathsf{tr}(r)))\}

(14)

Note that if
\mathsf{v}
is a visibility relation associated to an axiom from
\textup{{SER}},\textup{{SI}},\textup{{PC}},\textup{{RA}}
and
RC
isolation levels, transactions
t_{\mathsf{v}}^{x}
and
t_{x}^{r}
are well-defined as
\mathsf{v}(\textup{{{init}}},r,x)
holds.
Thus, let
\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}=\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}\cup\{(t_{x}^{r},r)\ |\ \hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}^{-1}(r)\uparrow\}
and
\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}=\bigcup_{x\in\mathsf{Keys}}\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}
. As
\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}^{-1}
is a total function and
{\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}^{-1}(r)}\ \mathsf{writes}\ {x}
we can conclude that
\overline{h}={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}})}
is a full history.

We prove that
\overline{h}
is also a witness of
h
. For that, we show that for every read event
r
, every key
x
and every transaction
t
, if
(t,r)\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}
,
t\in\mathtt{0}_{x}^{r}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}})
. Two cases arise:
(t,r)\in\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}
and
(t,r)\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}\setminus\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}
. The first case is quite straightforward, as if
(t,r)\in\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}_{x}
, by Property (1) of
Lemma

29
,
t\in\mathtt{0}_{x}^{r}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}})
. The second case,
(t,r)\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}\setminus\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}
, is slightly more subtle. First, for every isolation level considered, if
(t,r)\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}
then
(t,\mathsf{tr}(r))\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}
. Next, as
checkConsistency
(h)
returns
\mathsf{true}
, the condition at line
5
does not hold. Hence, as
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}
is acyclic, we deduce that
(\mathsf{tr}(r),t)\not\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}
. In addition, as
(t,r)\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}\setminus\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}
,
\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}^{-1}(r)\uparrow
. By Property (2) of
Lemma

29
employed during
\hat{h}
’s construction, we deduce that
\mathtt{1}_{x}^{r}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}})=\emptyset
. In conclusion, as
(\mathsf{tr}(r),t)\not\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}
and
\mathtt{1}_{x}^{r}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}})=\emptyset
, we conclude that
t\in\mathtt{0}_{x}^{r}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}})
.

Finally, we prove that
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
witnesses that
\overline{h}
is consistent. Let
r
be a
\mathtt{read}
event,
x
be a key and
t_{1},t_{2}
be transactions s.t.
(t_{1},r)\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}
and
{t_{2}}\ \mathsf{writes}\ {x}
. We prove that if there exists
\mathsf{v}\in\mathsf{vis}(\mathsf{iso}(\overline{h})(\mathsf{tr}(r)))
s.t.
\mathsf{v}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}})(t_{2},r,x)
holds in
\overline{h}
then
(t_{2},t_{1})\in{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
; which by
Definition

6
, we know it implies that
\overline{h}
is consistent. Note that if
(t_{1},r)\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}\setminus\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}

t_{x}^{r}
the statement immediately holds; so we can assume without loss of generality that
(t_{1},r)\in\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}_{x}
.

First, we note that proving that whenever
\mathsf{v}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}})(t_{2},r,x)
holds in
\overline{h}
, then

is equivalent to prove that whenever

holds in
\overline{h}
, then
t_{1}\not\in T_{i-1}
; where

is the index of the transaction in

s.t.
t_{2}=t_{i}
.

For every
i,1\leq i\leq|T|

P_{i-1}\triangleright_{t_{i}}P_{i}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}}
. Thus, by
Definition

6
, it suffices to show that for every read event

,
C_{\mathsf{iso}(h)(\mathsf{tr}(r))}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}})(r)
holds. For that, let
\hat{{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}}=\mathsf{FIX}(\lambda\mathit{R}:\textup{{saturate}}(\hat{h},\mathit{R}))({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})^{+}
be the partial commit order implied by

.

As
\mathsf{iso}(h)
is composed of
\{\textup{{SER}},\textup{{SI}},\textup{{PC}},\textup{{RA}},\textup{{RC}}\}

P_{i-1}\triangleright_{t_{2}}P_{i}
, by Property

of

, that there exists

\mathsf{iso}(\overline{h})(\mathsf{tr}(r))=\textup{{SI}}

and a key

\mathsf{\textsf{Prefix}}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}})(t_{2},r,x)

there exist

•

\mathsf{iso}(\overline{h})(\mathsf{tr}(r))=\textup{{PC}}
: In this case,
\mathsf{\textsf{Prefix}}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}})(t_{2},r,x)

there exist

\mathsf{\textsf{Prefix}}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{co}})(t_{2},r,x)

and

\mathsf{iso}(\overline{h})(\mathsf{tr}(r))=\textup{{RA}}

14
, if
(t_{2},\mathsf{tr}(r))\in\overline{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}\setminus({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup\hat{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}});{\color[rgb]{0.7695,0.1172,0.2266}\mathsf{po}}^{*}

,

0.B.5
Proof of
Theorem

5.2

See
5.2

The proof of Theorem
5.2
is split in two Lemmas: Lemma

analyzes the complexity of Algorithm
4
while Lemma

relies on the previous result to conclude the complexity of Algorithm

.

Algorithm 5

Checking if
P\triangleright_{t}(P\cup\{t\})
holds in
h

1:

function

isConsistentExtension
(
{h}={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
,
P={({T_{P},M_{P}})}
,
t
)

2:

\triangleright
We assume
t\not\in T_{P}
.

3:

let
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}=\mathsf{FIX}(\lambda\mathit{R}:\textup{{saturate}}(h,\mathit{R}))({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}})^{+}

if

s.t.

Lemma 31

Let
h={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
be a history,
P={({T_{P},M_{P}})}
be a consistent prefix of
h
and
t\in T\setminus T_{P}
be a transaction.
Algorithm

5
returns
\mathsf{true}
if and only if
P\triangleright_{t}(P\cup\{t\})
.

Proof

Clearly,
P\cup\{t\}
is an extension of
P
.
\textup{{isConsistentExtension}}(h,P,t)
returns
\mathsf{true}
if and only if conditions at lines
4
and lines
6
in
Algorithm

5
hold. This is equivalent to respectively satisfy Properties
1
and
2
of
Definition

10
. By
Definition

10
, this is equivalent to
P\triangleright_{t}(P\cup\{t\})
.

∎

Lemma 32

Let
h={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
be a history and
k\in\mathbb{N}
be a bound in
\mathsf{iso}(h)
. For any consistent prefix
P={({T_{P},M_{P}})}
of
h
and any transaction
t\in T\setminus T_{P}
, Algorithm
5
runs in
\mathcal{O}(|h|^{k+3})
.

Proof

We analyze the cost of
Algorithm

5
. First, as
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}\subseteq T\times T
, by
Lemma

7
, line
3
runs in
\mathcal{O}(|h|^{2}\cdot|h|^{k+1})
. Next, the condition at line
4
can be checked in
\mathcal{O}(|T|)
. Finally, the condition at line
6
can be checked in
\mathcal{O}(|T|\cdot k\cdot U)
; where
U
is an upper-bound on the complexity of checking
\mathsf{vp}_{v}^{P}(t,r)
. With the aid of
Lemma

6
, we deduce that
U\in\mathcal{O}(|h|^{k-2})
. Altogether, we conclude that
Algorithm

5
runs in
\mathcal{O}(|h|^{k+3})
.

∎

Lemma 33

Let
h={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
be a client history. If
\mathsf{iso}(h)
is composed of
\{\textup{{SER}},\textup{{SI}},\textup{{PC}},\textup{{RA}},\textup{{RC}}\}
isolation levels, then
5
is a bound of
\mathsf{iso}(h)
. Moreover, if no transaction has
SI
as isolation,
4
is a bound on
\mathsf{iso}(h)
.

Proof

Let
h
be a history as described in the hypothesis. First, all isolation levels in the set
\{\textup{{SER}},\textup{{SI}},\textup{{PC}},\textup{{RA}},\textup{{RC}}\}
employ at most two axioms. Moreover, every axiom described employs at most
5
quantifiers: three universal quantifiers and at most two existential quantifiers. Hence,
5
is a bound on
\mathsf{iso}(h)
. Note that
Conflict
is the only axiom employing two existential quantifiers; so if no transaction employs
SI
,
4
bounds
\mathsf{iso}(h)
.

∎

Lemma 34

Let
h={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
be a client history whose isolation configuration is composed of
\{\textup{{SER}},\textup{{SI}},\textup{{PC}},\textup{{RA}},\textup{{RC}}\}
isolation levels. Algorithm
4
runs in
\mathcal{O}(|h|^{\mathtt{width}(h)+9}\cdot\mathtt{width}(h)^{|\mathsf{Keys}|})
. Moreover, if no transaction has
SI
as isolation level, Algorithm
4
runs in
\mathcal{O}(|h|^{\mathtt{width}(h)+8})
.

Proof

For proving the result, we focus only on prefixes that are computable by Algorithm
4
. Let
h={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
be a history. A prefix
P
of
h
is
computable
if either
P=\emptyset
or there exist a transaction
t
and a prefix
P^{\prime}
s.t.
P=P^{\prime}\cup\{t\}
and
P^{\prime}
is computable.

Intuitively, computable prefixes represent recursive calls of Algorithm
4
when employed by Algorithm
3
. Indeed, Algorithm
3
only employs Algorithm
4
at lines
7
and
11
. In both cases,
P^{\prime}=\emptyset
is the initial call to Algorithm
4
. Moreover, the condition at line
3
justifies the recursive definition.

On one hand, we observe that any call to Algorithm
4
is associated to a computable prefix and on the other hand, Algorithm
4
does not explore two equivalent computable prefix thanks to the global variable
\mathtt{seen}
(line
4
). Therefore, Algorithm
4
runs in
\mathcal{O}(N\cdot U)
; where
N
is the number of distinct equivalence class of prefixes of
h
and
U
is an upper-bound on the running time of Algorithm
4
on a fixed prefix without doing any recursive call.

We first compute an upper-bound of
N
. For any computable prefix
P
, we can deduce by induction on the length of
P
that there exists transactions
t_{i}\in T_{P}
and a collection of computable prefixes of
h
,
P_{i}={({T_{i},M_{i}})}
and transactions
t_{i}
s.t.
P_{|T_{P}|}=P
,
P_{i}=P_{i-1}\cup\{t_{i}\}
and
P_{i-1}\triangleright_{t_{i}}P_{i}
; where
P_{0}=\emptyset
and
0<i\leq|T_{P}|
. The base case is immediate as
|T_{P}|=0
implies that
T^{\prime}=\emptyset
while the inductive step can be simply obtained by applying the recursive definition of computable prefix.

Let
P={({T_{P},M_{P}})}
be in what follows a computable prefix of
h
. We observe that both
T_{P}
and
M_{P}
are determined by its
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
-maximal transactions. Let
t,t^{\prime}\in T
be a pair of transactions s.t.
(t,t^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
and
t^{\prime}\in T_{P}
. As
t^{\prime}\in T_{P}
there must exist an index
i,1\leq i\leq|T_{P}|
s.t.
P_{i}=P_{i-1}\cup\{t^{\prime}\}
. Therefore, as
P_{i-1}\triangleright_{t^{\prime}}P_{i-1}
,
t\in T_{i-1}\subseteq T_{P}
. In particular, if
t^{\prime}
is a
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
-maximal transaction in
T_{P}
, all its
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
-predecessors are also contained in
T_{P}
; and hence,
T_{P}
can be characterized by its
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
-maximal transactions. Moreover, by induction on the length of
P
we can prove that for every key
x
,
M_{P}(x)
is a
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}
-maximal transaction: the base case,
|T_{P}|=0
is immediate while the inductive step is obtained by the definition of
P\cup\{t^{\prime\prime}\},t^{\prime\prime}\not\in T_{P}
. Hence, the number of computable prefixes of a history is in
\mathcal{O}(|T|^{\mathtt{width}(h)}\cdot{\mathtt{width}(h)}^{|\mathsf{Keys}|})
. Thus,
N\in\mathcal{O}(|h|^{\mathtt{width}(h)}\cdot\mathtt{width}(h)^{|\mathsf{Keys}|})
. Moreover, if no transaction employs
SI
as isolation level, prefixes with identical transaction set coincide. Hence, in such case,
N\in\mathcal{O}(|h|^{\mathtt{width}(h)})
.

We conclude the proof bounding
U
. If
|T_{P}|=|T|
, Algorithm
4
runs in
\mathcal{O}(1)
; so we can assume without loss of generality that
|T_{P}|\neq|T|
. In such case,
U
represent the cost of executing lines
3
-
7
in
Algorithm

4
. Thus,
U\in\mathcal{O}((|T|-|T_{P}|)\cdot V)
; where
V
is the cost of checking
P\triangleright_{t}(P\cup\{t\})
for a transaction
t\in T\setminus T_{P}
. By
Lemma

31
, Algorithm
5
can check if
P\triangleright_{t}(P\cup\{t\})
and thanks to
Lemma

32
, Algorithm
5
runs in
\mathcal{O}(|h|^{k+3})
; where
k
is a bound on
\mathsf{iso}(h)
. Thus,
U\in\mathcal{O}(|h|^{k+4})
.

Thanks to Lemma
33
, we conclude that
Algorithm

4
runs in
\mathcal{O}(|h|^{\mathtt{width}(h)+9}\cdot\mathtt{width}(h)^{|\mathsf{Keys}|})
and, if no transaction employs
SI
as isolation level, then it runs in
\mathcal{O}(|h|^{\mathtt{width}(h)+8})
.

∎

Lemma 35

Let
h={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
be a client history whose isolation configuration is composed of
\{\textup{{SER}},\textup{{SI}},\textup{{PC}},\textup{{RA}},\textup{{RC}}\}
isolation levels. Algorithm
3
runs in
\mathcal{O}(|h|^{\mathtt{\#conf}(h)+\mathtt{width}(h)+9}\cdot\mathtt{width}(h)^{|\mathsf{Keys}|})
. Moreover, if no transaction has
SI
as isolation level, Algorithm
3
runs in
\mathcal{O}(|h|^{\mathtt{\#conf}(h)+\mathtt{width}(h)+8})
.

Proof

Let
h={({T,{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}})}
be a history satisfying the hypothesis of the Lemma. We decompose our analysis in two sections, the first one where we analyze the complexity of executing lines
2
-
4
and second one where we analyze the complexity of executing lines
5
-
12
. We observe that by
Lemma

33
,
5
is a bound on
\mathsf{iso}(h)
.

In line
2
,
Algorithm

3
computes
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}
. On one hand, computing
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}})^{+}
is in
\mathcal{O}(|T|^{3})
. On the other hand, as
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}\subseteq T\times T
and by Lemma
7
, executing
\textup{{saturate}}(h,({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}})^{+})
is in
\mathcal{O}(|h|^{6})
; we deduce that computing
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}
after compute
({\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}})^{+}
is in
\mathcal{O}(|h|^{8})
.

In line
3
,
Algorithm

3
computes
E_{h}
. As
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}
is acyclic, for a given key
x
and transaction
t
,
\mathtt{value}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}}(t,x)\in\mathcal{O}(|T|)
. Therefore, computing
\mathtt{1}_{x}^{r}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}})
is in
\mathcal{O}(|T|)
as we assume that for every
r\in\mathsf{Rows}
, computing
\mathtt{WHERE}(r)(v)\in\mathcal{O}(1)
. Thus, computing
E_{h}
is in
\mathcal{O}(|h|^{3})
.

Finally, in line
4
,
Algorithm

3
computes
X_{h}
. Note that
X_{h}
can be seen is a
\bigtimes_{(r,x)\in E_{h}}\mathtt{0}_{x}^{r}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}})
. Computing each
\mathtt{0}_{x}^{r}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}})
set is in
\mathcal{O}(|T|)
; so computing all of them is in
\mathcal{O}(|T|\cdot|E_{h}|)
. As each set
\mathtt{0}_{x}^{r}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}})
is a subset of
T
, applying the cartesian-product definition of
X_{h}
we can compute
X_{h}
in
\mathcal{O}(|T|^{|E_{h}|})
. Therefore, as
|E_{h}|=\mathtt{\#conf}(h)
, we conclude that computing
X_{h}
is in
\mathcal{O}(|h|\cdot\mathtt{\#conf}(h)+|h|^{\mathtt{\#conf}(h)})
and that
|X_{h}|\in\mathcal{O}(|h|^{\mathtt{\#conf}(h)})
. Altogether, as
\mathtt{\#conf}(h)\leq|T|^{2}
, we deduce that computing lines
2
-
4
of Algorithm
3
is in
\mathcal{O}(|h|^{8}+|h|^{\mathtt{\#conf}(h)})
.

Next, we analyze the complexity of executing lines
5
-
12
. Four disjoint cases arise, one per boolean condition in
Algorithm

3
. The first one, checking if
{\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}}
is cyclic (line
5
), is in
\mathcal{O}(|h|)
. The second one, checking if
\exists(r,x)\in E_{h}
s.t.
\mathtt{0}_{x}^{r}({\color[rgb]{0.7031,0.4453,0}\mathsf{pco}})=\emptyset
(line
6
), clearly runs in
\mathcal{O}(\mathtt{\#conf}(h)\cdot|h|)
. The third one, checking if
E_{h}=\emptyset
and executing
Algorithm

4
is in
\mathcal{O}(\mathtt{\#conf}(h)+|h|^{\mathtt{width}(h)+9}\cdot\mathtt{width}(h)^{|\mathsf{Keys}|})
thanks to
Lemma

34
.

Finally we analyze the last case, computing an extension of
h
for each mapping in
X_{h}
and then executing
Algorithm

3
(lines
8
-
12
). On one hand, computing each history is in
\mathcal{O}(|h|^{3})
as we require to define both
{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}\mathsf{so}}\subseteq T\times T
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}\mathsf{wr}}\subseteq\mathsf{Keys}\times T\times T
. On the other hand, as the size of each extension of
h
is in
\mathcal{O}(|h|)
, executing
Algorithm

3
for a given history is in
\mathcal{O}(|X_{h}|\lx@nobreakspace\cdot|h|^{\mathtt{width}(h)+9}\cdot\mathtt{width}(h)^{|\mathsf{Keys}|})
thanks again to
Lemma

34
. Altogether, for each mapping
f\in X_{h}
, executing lines
10
-
11
is in
\mathcal{O}(|h|^{\mathtt{width}(h)+9}\cdot\mathtt{width}(h)^{|\mathsf{Keys}|})
. As
|X_{h}|\in\mathcal{O}(|h|^{\mathtt{\#conf}(h)})
, we conclude that executing this last case is in
\mathcal{O}(|h|^{\mathtt{\#conf}(h)+\mathtt{width}(h)+9}\cdot\mathtt{width}(h)^{|\mathsf{Keys}|})
.

We then conclude that Algorithm
3
runs in
\mathcal{O}(|h|^{\mathtt{\#conf}(h)+\mathtt{width}(h)+9}\cdot\mathtt{width}(h)^{|\mathsf{Keys}|})
. Moreover, if no transaction employs
SI
as isolation level,
Lemma

34
allows us to deduce that in such case
Algorithm

3
runs in
\mathcal{O}(|h|^{\mathtt{\#conf}(h)+\mathtt{width}(h)+9})
.

∎
